# Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol

<div class="ltx_authors">

<div id="abstract1" class="ltx_abstract">

###### Abstract

Recent advances in LLM based agent systems have shown promise in
tackling complex, long horizon tasks. However, existing agent protocols
(e.g., A2A and MCP) under specify cross entity lifecycle and context
management, version tracking, and evolution safe update interfaces,
which encourages monolithic compositions and brittle glue code. We
introduce
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">Autogenesis
Protocol (AGP)</span>, a self evolution protocol that decouples what
evolves from how evolution occurs. Its Resource Substrate Protocol Layer
(RSPL) models prompts, agents, tools, environments, and memory as
protocol registered
resources<span class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnote"><sup>1</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>1</sup><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_note">1</span>Unless
otherwise specified, resources refer to instances of the five RSPL
entity types: *prompt*, *agent*, *tool/MCP/skill*, *environment*,
*memory* with agent *outputs/solutions*. *Tool* refers to local
code-based tools, MCP tools, and skills.</span></span></span> with
explicit state, lifecycle, and versioned interfaces. Its Self Evolution
Protocol Layer (SEPL) specifies a closed loop operator interface for
proposing, assessing, and committing improvements with auditable lineage
and rollback. Building on
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span>, we
present
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">Autogenesis
System (AGS)</span>, a self-evolving multi-agent system that dynamically
instantiates, retrieves, and refines protocol-registered resources
during execution. We evaluate
<span class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGS</span> on
multiple challenging benchmarks that require long horizon planning and
tool use across heterogeneous resources. The results demonstrate
consistent improvements over strong baselines, supporting the
effectiveness of agent resource management and closed loop self
evolution.

</div>

<span id="footnotex6"
class="ltx_note ltx_role_footnotetext"><sup>†</sup><span class="ltx_note_outer"><span class="ltx_note_content"><sup>†</sup><span class="ltx_note_type">footnotetext:
</span><sup>∗</sup>Equal contribution. First Author Contact:
<span id="footnotex6.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">zhangwent963@gmail.com</span>
 <sup>†</sup>Corresponding authors.</span></span></span>

<div id="S1" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">1 </span>Introduction

<div id="S1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Recent advances in LLM-based agent systems have demonstrated significant
potential in tackling complex, long-horizon
tasks Yao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref"
title="React: synergizing reasoning and acting in language models">2022</a>);
Wei<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref"
title="Chain-of-thought prompting elicits reasoning in large language models">2022</a>);
Brown<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib27" class="ltx_ref"
title="Language models are few-shot learners">2020</a>), yet static
designs often prove insufficient against the diversity and stochasticity
of real-world environments. Endowing agents with self-evolution
capabilities has thus emerged as a critical avenue toward robust
autonomy. However, existing implementations remain largely fragmented
and ad hoc: components such as prompts, tools, and memory are tightly
coupled to agent logic, shared standards are absent, and the lack of
explicit lifecycle management and safe update interfaces introduces
significant risks of runtime instability, preventing self-evolution from
being composable, auditable, or systematically reproducible.

</div>

<div id="S1.p2" class="ltx_para">

Although protocols such as MCP Anthropic
(<a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Introduction to agent skills">2025b</a>) and A2A Google
(<a href="#bib.bib12" class="ltx_ref"
title="A2A: a new era of agent interoperability">2025</a>) have
standardized connectivity for model-tool invocation and inter-agent
communication, they operate solely at the level of invocation and
message passing, leaving internal resource states opaque. Neither
provides mechanisms for lifecycle management, version lineage, or
controlled state mutation, which are precisely the requirements of a
closed-loop evolutionary system. Bridging this gap calls for a dedicated
protocol addressing three essential properties: <span id="S1.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Decoupling</span>, so that resources such
as prompts, tools, and memory are managed as independent entities rather
than tightly coupled code; <span id="S1.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Safety & Auditability</span>, through
strict version control and rollback to ensure every evolutionary step is
traceable and reversible; and <span id="S1.p2.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Formalism</span>, via standardized
operators (e.g., reflect, propose, verify) that convert heuristic
modifications into a rigorous control loop.

</div>

<div id="S1.p3" class="ltx_para">

To address these challenges, we propose <span id="S1.p3.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">Autogenesis Protocol
(AGP)</span>, a two-layer protocol architecture that formally decouples
the evolutionary substrate from the evolutionary logic. The central
design principle is to standardize resource representations, enabling
uniform application of optimization
algorithms Yuksekgonul<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref"
title="Optimizing generative ai by backpropagating language model feedback">2025</a>);
Shao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref"
title="Deepseekmath: pushing the limits of mathematical reasoning in open language models">2024</a>);
Hu (<a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reinforce++: a simple and efficient approach for aligning large language models">2025b</a>)
across heterogeneous agent components. The <span id="S1.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Resource Substrate Protocol Layer
(RSPL)</span> constitutes the substrate of evolution, modeling prompts,
agents, tools, environments, and memory systems as protocol-registered
resources endowed with explicit state, lifecycle, and versioned
interfaces, thereby rendering them well-defined objects amenable to
systematic observation and controlled manipulation. The
<span id="S1.p3.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Self-Evolution
Protocol Layer (SEPL)</span> establishes a closed-loop operator
interface grounded in control theory, specifying a set of atomic
operators that formally govern the evolution cycle and guarantee that
every self-modification is fully auditable and subject to strict safety
constraints. Building upon this protocol, we instantiate
<span id="S1.p3.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">Autogenesis System
(AGS)</span>, a self-evolving multi-agent system system that dynamically
registers, retrieves, and refines protocol resources at runtime.
Empirical evaluation on a suite of challenging benchmarks, including
GPQA Rein<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref"
title="GPQA: a graduate-level google-proof q&amp;a benchmark">2024</a>),
AIME, GAIA Mialon<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib6" class="ltx_ref"
title="Gaia: a benchmark for general ai assistants">2023</a>),
HLE (Phan<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib44" class="ltx_ref"
title="Humanity’s last exam">2025</a>), and
LeetCode <a href="#bib.bib11" class="ltx_ref"
title="LeetCode online judge">LeetCode</a> , demonstrates that
<span id="S1.p3.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGS</span> achieves
consistent and substantial improvements over strong baselines,
validating the efficacy of principled resource management and
closed-loop self-evolution. The contributions of this work are
threefold:

- <span id="S1.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  We propose <span id="S1.I1.i1.p1.1.1"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">Autogenesis Protocol
  (AGP)</span>, a two-layer self-evolution protocol decoupling
  evolutionary substrate from logic. RSPL endows resources with explicit
  state, lifecycle, and versioned interfaces; SEPL governs the evolution
  cycle via a closed-loop operator interface with auditable lineage and
  rollback.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  We present <span id="S1.I1.i2.p1.1.1"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">Autogenesis System
  (AGS)</span>, a self-evolving multi-agent system system that
  dynamically registers, retrieves, and refines protocol resources at
  runtime, demonstrating the practical viability of protocol-driven
  self-evolution.

  </div>
- <span id="S1.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="S1.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  We conduct empirical evaluation on five challenging benchmarks (GPQA,
  AIME, GAIA, HLE, and LeetCode), demonstrating consistent and
  substantial improvements over strong baselines and validating the
  efficacy of principled resource management and closed-loop evolution.

  </div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">2 </span>Related Work

<div id="S2.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.1 </span>LLM-based Agent Systems and Protocols

<div id="S2.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

LLM-based agent systems have demonstrated strong capabilities in
complex, long-horizon tasks requiring multi-step reasoning and external
tool interaction Rein<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib7" class="ltx_ref"
title="GPQA: a graduate-level google-proof q&amp;a benchmark">2024</a>);
Mialon<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib6" class="ltx_ref"
title="Gaia: a benchmark for general ai assistants">2023</a>);
Yao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib13" class="ltx_ref"
title="React: synergizing reasoning and acting in language models">2022</a>);
Wei<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib15" class="ltx_ref"
title="Chain-of-thought prompting elicits reasoning in large language models">2022</a>);
Schick<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref"
title="Toolformer: language models can teach themselves to use tools">2023</a>),
with LLMs serving as centralized decision-making modules that decompose
tasks and invoke tools to act on the environment. However, most existing
frameworks treat prompts, tools, and memory as tightly coupled internal
components: tools are manually curated fixed modules integrated directly
into the agent pipeline Qin<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span> (<a href="#bib.bib25" class="ltx_ref"
title="Toolllm: facilitating large language models to master 16000+ real-world apis">2023</a>);
Schick<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib14" class="ltx_ref"
title="Toolformer: language models can teach themselves to use tools">2023</a>);
Chen<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib26" class="ltx_ref"
title="Evaluating large language models trained on code">2021</a>),
limiting systematic reuse and controlled adaptation as task requirements
evolve. Efforts such as Anthropic’s MCP Anthropic
(<a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Equipping agents for the real world with agent skills">2025a</a>)
and Google’s A2A protocol have standardized model-tool interaction and
inter-agent communication at the level of invocation and message
passing, but leave the internal state of agents and resources opaque,
providing no mechanisms for managing resource lifecycles, tracking
version lineage, or constraining state mutations over time. In contrast,
our approach treats prompts, agents, local code-based tools, MCP tools,
and skills Anthropic (<a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Introduction to agent skills">2025b</a>) as protocol-registered
entities endowed with explicit interfaces and versioned state, thereby
supporting dynamic instantiation, controlled refinement, and auditable
evolution throughout execution.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S2.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">2.2 </span>Self-Evolution and Optimization of Agent Components

<div id="S2.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

A parallel line of work investigates iterative agent improvement via
gradient-free methods such as
TextGrad Yuksekgonul<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref"
title="Optimizing generative ai by backpropagating language model feedback">2025</a>),
which treat natural language feedback as a gradient
signal Pryzant<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib23" class="ltx_ref"
title="Automatic prompt optimization with &quot;gradient descent&quot; and beam search">2023</a>);
Zhou<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib24" class="ltx_ref"
title="Large language models are human-level prompt engineers">2022</a>),
and reinforcement learning approaches such as Reinforce++ Hu
(<a href="#bib.bib5" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reinforce++: a simple and efficient approach for aligning large language models">2025a</a>)
and GRPO Shao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref"
title="Deepseekmath: pushing the limits of mathematical reasoning in open language models">2024</a>),
which frame agent components as policies optimized via evaluation
rewards Shinn<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib19" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reflexion: language agents with verbal reinforcement learning">2023</a>);
Madaan<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib21" class="ltx_ref"
title="Self-refine: iterative refinement with self-feedback">2023</a>);
Zelikman<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib20" class="ltx_ref"
title="Star: bootstrapping reasoning with reasoning">2022</a>). More
recent frameworks such as
EvoAgentX Wang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Evoagentx: an automated framework for evolving agentic workflows">2025</a>)
and Hermes Agent NousResearch (<a href="#bib.bib2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Hermes Agent: A Self-Improving Open-Source Agent Framework">2025</a>)
further pursue self-evolving agent workflows, autonomously constructing
and refining multi-agent pipelines or skill libraries from interaction
history. Despite this progress, these approaches focus on optimizing a
narrow subset of agent components, typically prompts or task workflows,
and do not provide a unified abstraction for managing the full spectrum
of agent-internal entities including prompts, tools, and environments.
Updates are applied directly without lifecycle control, version
tracking, or rollback, precluding safe and auditable evolution. Our
approach addresses this limitation via a two-layer architecture that
exposes all agent components as protocol-registered resources governed
by a principled closed-loop operator interface.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">3 </span>Autogenesis Protocol

<figure id="S3.F1" class="ltx_figure">
<img src="2604.15034v5/x1.png" id="S3.F1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="647"
height="270" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S3.F1.2.1.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 1</span>: </span><span
id="S3.F1.3.2" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">The Autogenesis
protocol and system architecture.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="S3.p1" class="ltx_para">

This section describes the <span id="S3.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span>
specification, covering the resource substrate and the evolution
operator interface. The concrete system instantiation built upon this
protocol is presented in the next section. Despite growing interest in
self-evolving agents Gao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et
al.</span> (<a href="#bib.bib33" class="ltx_ref"
title="A survey of self-evolving agents: what, when, how, and where to evolve on the path to artificial super intelligence">2025</a>),
most systems remain engineered in an ad hoc manner and lack a shared
protocol standard that makes evolution composable, auditable, and
interoperable. As shown in <a href="#S3.F1" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 3 Autogenesis Protocol ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span>˜<span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>,, we introduce
<span id="S3.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span>, a
two-layer self-evolution protocol. The *Resource Substrate Protocol
Layer (RSPL)* specifies the evolvable substrate, namely which resources
may change and how they are represented, versioned, and accessed. The
*Self-Evolution Protocol Layer (SEPL)* specifies the evolution logic,
namely how updates are proposed, assessed, and committed through a safe
operator interface. Inspired by interface standardization efforts in
agent tooling, this separation cleanly decouples *what evolves* from
*how evolution occurs*, enabling modularity, traceability, and
safety-preserving evolution across components.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.1 </span>Layer 1: Resource Substrate Protocol Layer

<div id="S3.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

The Resource Substrate Protocol Layer (RSPL) defines the evolvable
substrate as a set of protocol-registered resources with explicit state,
lifecycle, and version lineage. We identify five entity types as a
minimal yet expressive common denominator across modern agent stacks,
providing a uniform target space on which SEPL can operate: (i)
*instructions* (<span id="S3.SS1.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text">Prompt</span>), (ii) *decision policies*
(<span id="S3.SS1.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text">Agent</span>), (iii)
*actuation interfaces* (<span id="S3.SS1.p1.1.6"
class="ltx_text">Tool</span>), encompassing local code-based tools, MCP
tools Anthropic (<a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Equipping agents for the real world with agent skills">2025a</a>),
and agent skills Anthropic (<a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Introduction to agent skills">2025b</a>), (iv) *task/world
dynamics* (<span id="S3.SS1.p1.1.8"
class="ltx_text">Environment</span>), and (v) *persistent state*
(<span id="S3.SS1.p1.1.10" class="ltx_text">Memory</span>). Crucially,
resources in RSPL are *passive*, meaning they encapsulate no
optimization logic, cannot self-modify, and change state only through
controlled operations mediated by interfaces and invoked by higher
layers. This separation decouples agent logic from task-specific
instructions and capability
bundles Wu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref"
title="Autogen: enabling next-gen llm applications via multi-agent conversations">2024</a>);
Hong<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref"
title="MetaGPT: meta programming for a multi-agent collaborative framework">2023</a>);
Chen<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib18" class="ltx_ref"
title="Agentverse: facilitating multi-agent collaboration and exploring emergent behaviors">2023</a>),
enabling the same policy to be deployed across tasks with different
resource configurations.

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">3.1.1 </span>Infrastructure Services

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

A self-evolution protocol requires reliable foundational support in
which model access remains consistent as components are swapped, every
state transition is traceable and reversible, resources persist across
sessions and can be safely hot-swapped, and execution behavior is
observable for diagnosis and improvement. To meet these requirements,
RSPL provides four cross-cutting infrastructure services: (i) A
<span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">model
manager</span> standardizes LLM API calls across heterogeneous
providers, including Anthropic, OpenAI, Google, xAI, and OpenRouter, and
supports routing and fallback to ensure consistent model access as
resources evolve. (ii) A <span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">version manager</span> maintains
immutable snapshots and version lineage, enabling rollback, branching,
and auditability at every state transition. (iii) A
<span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.p1.1.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">dynamic
manager</span> handles serialization and hot-swapping of resource
configurations at runtime without restarting the agent system. (iv) A
<span id="S3.SS1.SSS1.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">trace
manager</span> captures fine-grained execution traces for
interpretability, debugging, and retrospective optimization.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">3.1.2 </span>Core Entities

<div id="S3.Thmtheorem1" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_definition">

###### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="S3.Thmtheorem1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Definition 3.1</span></span><span id="S3.Thmtheorem1.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"> </span>(Resource Entity)<span id="S3.Thmtheorem1.3.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span>

<div id="S3.Thmtheorem1.p1" class="ltx_para">

A resource entity and its type-level collection are defined as:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle e_{\tau,i}`$ | $`\displaystyle=(n_{\tau,i},\,d_{\tau,i},\,\phi_{\tau,i},\,g_{\tau,i},\,m_{\tau,i}),`$ |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equationgroup ltx_align_right">(1)</span> |
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\tau}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\{\,e_{\tau,i}\mid i\in\mathcal{I}_{\tau}\,\},`$ |  |  |

where
$`\mathcal{T}=\{\textsc{Prompt},\textsc{Agent},\textsc{Tool},\textsc{Env},\textsc{Mem}\}`$
is the set of RSPL entity types, $`\tau\in\mathcal{T}`$ indexes the
type, $`\mathcal{I}_{\tau}`$ is the index set for instances of type
$`\tau`$, and $`i\in\mathcal{I}_{\tau}`$ indexes an individual instance.
$`n_{\tau,i}`$ is a unique name, $`d_{\tau,i}`$ a short description,
$`\phi_{\tau,i}:\mathcal{X}_{\tau}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{\tau}`$ an
input-to-output mapping, $`g_{\tau,i}\in\{0,1\}`$ an evolvability
marker, and $`m_{\tau,i}`$ an auxiliary metadata dictionary.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

To support resource registration, unified management, and instantiation,
RSPL stores a serializable registration record for each resource
instance.

</div>

<div id="S3.Thmtheorem2" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_definition">

###### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="S3.Thmtheorem2.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Definition 3.2</span></span><span id="S3.Thmtheorem2.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"> </span>(Resource Registration Record)<span id="S3.Thmtheorem2.3.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span>

<div id="S3.Thmtheorem2.p1" class="ltx_para">

A resource registration record and its type-level collection can be
represented as:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle c_{\tau,i}`$ | $`\displaystyle=(e_{\tau,i},\,v_{\tau,i},\,\eta_{\tau,i},\,\theta_{\tau,i},\,\mathcal{F}_{\tau,i}),`$ |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equationgroup ltx_align_right">(2)</span> |
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\tau}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\{\,c_{\tau,i}\mid i\in\mathcal{I}_{\tau}\,\},`$ |  |  |

where $`\tau\in\mathcal{T}`$ indexes the entity type and
$`i\in\mathcal{I}_{\tau}`$ indexes an individual instance. Here
$`e_{\tau,i}`$ is the resource entity tuple defined in
<a href="#S3.Thmtheorem1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Definition 3.1 (Resource Entity). ‣ 3.1.2 Core Entities ‣ 3.1 Layer 1: Resource Substrate Protocol Layer ‣ 3 Autogenesis Protocol ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Definition</span>˜<span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.1</span></a>, $`v_{\tau,i}\in\mathbb{V}`$
is a version string, $`\eta_{\tau,i}`$ is an implementation descriptor
(e.g., import path, class definition, or source-code string),
$`\theta_{\tau,i}`$ are instantiation parameters (e.g., constructor
arguments), and $`\mathcal{F}_{\tau,i}`$ is a set of exported
representations used by LLMs to interact with the resource (e.g.,
function-calling schema, plain text, and structured argument schema).

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.Thmtheorem3" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_definition">

###### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="S3.Thmtheorem3.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Definition 3.3</span></span><span id="S3.Thmtheorem3.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"> </span>(Protocol-registered resource)<span id="S3.Thmtheorem3.3.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span>

<div id="S3.Thmtheorem3.p1" class="ltx_para">

For each entity type $`\tau`$, let $`\mathcal{R}_{\tau}`$ denote the
type-specific registry of protocol-registered resources, and let
$`\mathcal{R}=\bigcup_{\tau}\mathcal{R}_{\tau}`$ denote the
corresponding global registry. RSPL associates each entity type $`\tau`$
with a dedicated context manager $`\mathcal{M}_{\tau}`$ and a
server-exposed interface $`\mathcal{A}_{\tau}`$. We represent the
type-level registered resource as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\footnotesize r_{\tau}=(\mathcal{C}_{\tau},\;\mathcal{M}_{\tau},\;\mathcal{A}_{\tau}),
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(3)</span> |

where each $`c_{\tau,i}\in\mathcal{C}_{\tau}`$ denotes a registration
record as defined in <a href="#S3.Thmtheorem2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Definition 3.2 (Resource Registration Record). ‣ 3.1.2 Core Entities ‣ 3.1 Layer 1: Resource Substrate Protocol Layer ‣ 3 Autogenesis Protocol ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Definition</span>˜<span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3.2</span></a>. The context manager
$`\mathcal{M}_{\tau}`$ maintains the record collection
$`\mathcal{C}_{\tau}`$ and the version lineage associated with type
$`\tau`$, while implementing lifecycle and update operations over these
records. The server-exposed interface $`\mathcal{A}_{\tau}`$
encapsulates $`\mathcal{M}_{\tau}`$ and provides a unified external
interface by delegating incoming requests to the corresponding
context-manager routines.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS1.SSS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S3.SS1.SSS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Context
manager and server interface.</span> Each resource type is governed by a
context manager, which serves as the management plane. It maintains a
registry of materialized resources, preserves versioned histories for
restoration, and supports *contract generation* by producing a
consolidated capability specification. This specification reduces prompt
bloat and enables *context engineering* through controlled injection.
For tools, the contract takes a <span id="S3.SS1.SSS2.p2.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">skills.md</span>-style
form (Anthropic, <a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Introduction to agent skills">2025b</a>) that enumerates actions,
arguments, and usage constraints. The context-manager API provides
operators for lifecycle management (<span id="S3.SS1.SSS2.p2.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">init</span>,
<span id="S3.SS1.SSS2.p2.1.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">build</span>), retrieval
(<span id="S3.SS1.SSS2.p2.1.7"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">list</span>,
<span id="S3.SS1.SSS2.p2.1.8"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">get</span>), versioning
(<span id="S3.SS1.SSS2.p2.1.9"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">update</span>,
<span id="S3.SS1.SSS2.p2.1.10"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">restore</span>), execution
(<span id="S3.SS1.SSS2.p2.1.11"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">run</span>), and serialization
(<span id="S3.SS1.SSS2.p2.1.12"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">save_to_json</span>,
<span id="S3.SS1.SSS2.p2.1.13"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">load_from_json</span>,
<span id="S3.SS1.SSS2.p2.1.14"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">save_contract</span>,
<span id="S3.SS1.SSS2.p2.1.15"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">load_contract</span>). The server
interface encapsulates this internal complexity behind a uniform set of
endpoints with consistent request and response semantics, providing a
single control plane for safe and version-aware interactions with RSPL
resources. Full specifications are in
<a href="#A5.SS2.SSS2" class="ltx_ref"
title="E.2.2 Context Manager ‣ E.2 Layer 1: Resource Substrate Protocol Layer ‣ Appendix E Details of Self-Evolution Protocol ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span>˜<span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">E.2.2</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">3.2 </span>Layer 2: Self-Evolution Protocol Layer (SEPL)

<div id="S3.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

The Self-Evolution Protocol Layer (SEPL) formalizes agentic system
evolution as a generalized optimization problem over a heterogeneous
state space, modeling evolutionary dynamics as a state transition
function governed by a strictly typed operator algebra. By mediating all
state mutations through standardized RSPL interfaces, SEPL guarantees
that evolution is traceable, reversible, and safe-by-construction. While
this paper focuses on the reflection-driven optimizer as the primary
instantiation, the same state manipulation primitives also accommodate
textual-gradient methods such as
TextGrad (Yuksekgonul<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref"
title="Optimizing generative ai by backpropagating language model feedback">2025</a>)
and reinforcement learning approaches such as
GRPO (Shao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref"
title="Deepseekmath: pushing the limits of mathematical reasoning in open language models">2024</a>)
and Reinforce++ (Hu, <a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reinforce++: a simple and efficient approach for aligning large language models">2025b</a>).

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">3.2.1 </span>Evolvable Variables

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

To transition from heuristic adaptation to a systematic evolution
protocol, we introduce the concept of *variable lifting*. This
abstraction projects discrete, heterogeneous RSPL resources (e.g., tool
code, system prompts) onto a unified representation of evolvable
variables. This formalism offers significant theoretical advantages by
homogenizing the interaction surface for evolutionary operators and
rigorously delineating the trainable subspace via an explicit
learnability mask.

</div>

<div id="S3.Thmtheorem4" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_definition">

###### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="S3.Thmtheorem4.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Definition 3.4</span></span><span id="S3.Thmtheorem4.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"> </span>(Evolvable Variable Set)<span id="S3.Thmtheorem4.3.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span>

<div id="S3.Thmtheorem4.p1" class="ltx_para">

We define the universal set of evolvable variables as
$`\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}=\bigl(\bigcup_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{E}_{\tau}\bigr)\cup\{y\}`$,
where $`\mathcal{E}_{\tau}`$ denotes the set of resource entities of
type $`\tau`$ governed by the RSPL. The element $`y`$ encapsulates
execution artifacts, specifically final outputs and reasoning traces,
which constitute the observational basis for retrospective optimization.
Furthermore, each variable $`v\in\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}`$ is
associated with a binary learnability constraint $`g_{v}\in\{0,1\}`$,
thereby strictly defining the trainable parameter subspace
$`\Theta=\{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}\mid g_{v}=1\}`$.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">3.2.2 </span>Operator Algebra

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

To systematically govern state transitions over
$`\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}`$, we introduce the notion of a *SEPL
operator*: a typed, composable function that reads the current evolvable
state together with auxiliary signals, produces an updated state, and
emits signals for downstream operators. Formalizing evolution as an
algebra of such operators ensures that every modification is
interface-mediated, auditable, and reversible, regardless of the
specific optimization strategy instantiated.

</div>

<div id="S3.Thmtheorem5" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_definition">

###### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="S3.Thmtheorem5.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Definition 3.5</span></span><span id="S3.Thmtheorem5.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"> </span>(SEPL Operator)<span id="S3.Thmtheorem5.3.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span>

<div id="S3.Thmtheorem5.p1" class="ltx_para">

Let $`\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}`$ be the evolvable variable set and
$`\mathcal{P}`$ a *message space* carrying auxiliary signals (e.g.,
traces, hypotheses, gradients, or reward signals) passed between
operators. A *SEPL operator* is a function

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\footnotesize f:\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}\times\mathcal{P}_{\text{in}}\;\rightarrow\;\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\text{evo}}\times\mathcal{P}_{\text{out}},
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(4)</span> |

where
$`\mathcal{P}_{\text{in}},\mathcal{P}_{\text{out}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}`$
are the incoming and outgoing message types, and
$`\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\text{evo}}`$ is the updated evolvable state.
Operators are *composable*: the output
$`(\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\text{evo}},\mathcal{P}_{\text{out}})`$ of one
operator serves as the input to the next, enabling the construction of
an evolutionary pipeline $`f_{n}\circ\cdots\circ f_{1}`$.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS3" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">3.2.3 </span>Evolutionary Loop

<div id="S3.SS2.SSS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Given an initial evolvable state $`\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}^{(0)}`$ and
an empty message $`\mathcal{P}^{(0)}=\emptyset`$, the evolutionary loop
at each iteration $`t`$ applies a sequence of operators
$`f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}`$ in composition:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\footnotesize\bigl(\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}^{(t+1)},\,\mathcal{P}^{(t+1)}\bigr)=(f_{n}\circ\cdots\circ f_{1})\bigl(\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}^{(t)},\,\mathcal{P}^{(t)}\bigr),
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(5)</span> |

where each $`f_{i}`$ reads the current state and incoming messages,
produces an updated state and outgoing messages consumed by $`f_{i+1}`$.
The loop repeats until convergence or budget exhaustion. By routing all
state mutations through RSPL interfaces, each transition is versioned
and reversible, guaranteeing that evolution is *grounded* in execution
data, *traceable* through versioned updates, and *safe-by-construction*.
For example, the reflection optimizer instantiates this loop with five
operators: <span id="S3.SS2.SSS3.p1.6.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Reflect</span> maps execution traces
and current state to causal failure hypotheses,
<span id="S3.SS2.SSS3.p1.6.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Select</span> identifies target
evolvable entities from the current state and hypotheses, generating
concrete modification proposals, <span id="S3.SS2.SSS3.p1.6.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Improve</span> applies proposals via
RSPL interfaces to yield a candidate state,
<span id="S3.SS2.SSS3.p1.6.7"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Evaluate</span> scores the candidate
against the objective and safety invariants, and
<span id="S3.SS2.SSS3.p1.6.8"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Commit</span> conditionally accepts
or rolls back the transition. Full pseudocode for all instantiations is
in <a href="#A5.SS3" class="ltx_ref"
title="E.3 Layer 2: Self-Evolution Protocol Layer ‣ Appendix E Details of Self-Evolution Protocol ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Section</span>˜<span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">E.3</span></a>.

</div>

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S4" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">4 </span>Autogenesis System

<div id="S4.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">4.1 </span>Autogenesis System Architecture

<div id="S4.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

As shown in <a href="#S3.F1" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 3 Autogenesis Protocol ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span>˜<span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>, building on
<span id="S4.SS1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span>, we
instantiate the two-layer protocol into <span id="S4.SS1.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGS</span>, a
self-evolving multi-agent system. A self-evolving system requires that
agents, tools, and coordination structures remain dynamically modifiable
at runtime, which is fundamentally incompatible with monolithic
controllers or hard-wired pipelines that tightly couple execution logic
to agent identity. To satisfy this requirement, we adopt a *bus
interaction model* Wu<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib17" class="ltx_ref"
title="Autogen: enabling next-gen llm applications via multi-agent conversations">2024</a>);
Hong<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>
(<a href="#bib.bib16" class="ltx_ref"
title="MetaGPT: meta programming for a multi-agent collaborative framework">2023</a>):
the planning agent and all sub-agents register as first-class
participants on a shared *Agent Bus*, and all inter-agent communication
is mediated exclusively through standardized bus messages. This
decoupling enables loose coupling, transparent observability, and
concurrent sub-agent execution, while allowing any participant to be
replaced or evolved without disrupting the rest of the system.
Throughout all configurations, prompts, tools, and agents are treated as
*first-class RSPL resources* with explicit lifecycle and version
lineage. The system operates through three interleaved mechanisms:

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Orchestration
via Plan Generation.</span> Upon receiving a task via the bus, the
planning agent is responsible solely for planning and coordination and
does not execute subtasks directly. It produces a structured
<span id="S4.SS1.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">plan.md</span> artifact comprising
five components: the original task description, a to-do list of subtask
steps each assigned to a designated sub-agent (e.g., deep researcher
agent, browser-use agent, deep analyzer agent and vibe coding agent), an
execution flowchart, a running execution history, and a final result
summary. The planning agent dispatches subtasks to the designated
sub-agents via the bus, executing independent subtasks concurrently and
dependent ones sequentially, and collects all results through the bus
before proceeding to the next round.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Concurrent
Sub-Agent Execution and Iterative Re-planning.</span> Upon receiving a
dispatched subtask, each sub-agent independently retrieves relevant
prompt and tool resources from the RSPL registry, executes tool calls,
and writes results and reasoning traces to shared memory. Multiple
sub-agents execute concurrently, as the bus decouples dispatch from
completion. Once a round concludes, the planning agent collects outputs
via the bus, updates <span id="S4.SS1.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">plan.md</span>, and determines
whether the task is complete or a further round of decomposition is
required. This collect-and-replan loop continues until the termination
condition is met. As a complementary pattern, <span id="S4.SS1.p3.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGS</span> also
supports *agent-as-tool* composition, in which a sub-agent is wrapped
behind a standard RSPL tool schema and invoked directly by a
tool-calling agent, enabling lightweight collaboration without bus-level
orchestration.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S4.SS1.p4.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Self-Evolution.</span> Interleaved with
the bus coordination loop, <span id="S4.SS1.p4.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGS</span> invokes the
SEPL evolutionary loop whenever execution traces signal correctable
failures or suboptimal performance. The loop applies a sequence of SEPL
operators to reflect, select, improve, evaluate, and commit resource
modifications as versioned RSPL transitions with auditable lineage and
rollback. As an example instantiation, the reflection optimizer
(Algorithm <a href="#alg1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Algorithm 1 ‣ E.3.4 Reflection Optimizer ‣ E.3 Layer 2: Self-Evolution Protocol Layer ‣ Appendix E Details of Self-Evolution Protocol ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>) reflects on execution traces
to derive causal failure hypotheses, generates modification proposals
(e.g., prompt text, tool source code, MCP configurations, or skill
definitions), and commits accepted updates only after evaluating
candidates against the task objective. Successful updates are
immediately available to all sub-agents in subsequent bus rounds,
ensuring that evolution remains traceable throughout the agent lifetime.

</div>

<div id="S4.SS1.p5" class="ltx_para">

Beyond the reflection optimizer, our implementation supports additional
optimization strategies that map naturally onto the same SEPL operator
interface. *TextGrad* (Yuksekgonul<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref"
title="Optimizing generative ai by backpropagating language model feedback">2025</a>)
instantiates the proposal and improvement operators as a
gradient-informed text editor, treating natural-language feedback as a
textual gradient applied to string variables. *Reinforce++ / GRPO* (Hu,
<a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reinforce++: a simple and efficient approach for aligning large language models">2025b</a>;
Shao<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref"
title="Deepseekmath: pushing the limits of mathematical reasoning in open language models">2024</a>;
Ouyang<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib28" class="ltx_ref"
title="Training language models to follow instructions with human feedback">2022</a>;
Ziegler<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib31" class="ltx_ref"
title="Fine-tuning language models from human preferences">2019</a>;
Schulman<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib30" class="ltx_ref"
title="Proximal policy optimization algorithms">2017</a>) adopt a
reinforcement-learning perspective, treating evolvable variables as
policies optimized via policy-gradient estimates against evaluation
rewards. These strategies demonstrate that SEPL is sufficiently general
to accommodate inference-time reflection optimization,
textual-gradient-based string updates, and reward-driven policy
optimization within a unified protocol.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">5 </span>Empirical Studies

<div id="S5.p1" class="ltx_para">

In this section, we present empirical results of deploying
<span id="S5.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGS</span> across
various challenging benchmarks with <span id="S5.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> to
demonstrate its comprehensive capabilities.

</div>

<div id="S5.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Benchmark
Instruction</span>. We organize our evaluation into three categories.
(i) <span id="S5.p2.1.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"> Scientific and
Mathematical Benchmarks.</span> <span id="S5.p2.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">GPQA-Diamond</span> (198 questions)
presents graduate-level STEM multiple-choice questions (biology,
chemistry, and physics) under a closed-book, non-retrieval protocol,
measuring deep scientific understanding and multi-step reasoning.
<span id="S5.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">AIME24</span> and
<span id="S5.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">AIME25</span> each
consist of 30 competition-level mathematics problems requiring exact
integer answers, measuring long-horizon symbolic reasoning and
arithmetic precision. (ii) <span id="S5.p2.1.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"> General Agent Benchmarks.</span>
<span id="S5.p2.1.7"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">GAIA</span> (Mialon<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal">
et al.</span>, <a href="#bib.bib6" class="ltx_ref"
title="Gaia: a benchmark for general ai assistants">2023</a>) includes a
Validation split (165 tasks) and a Test split (300 tasks), each
specifying a real-world, multi-step objective requiring planning and
tool use (e.g., web browsing, document operations), measured by
task-completion accuracy across three difficulty tiers.
<span id="S5.p2.1.8" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Humanity’s Last Exam
(HLE)</span> (Phan<span class="ltx_text ltx_bib_etal"> et al.</span>,
<a href="#bib.bib44" class="ltx_ref"
title="Humanity’s last exam">2025</a>) comprises extremely difficult
expert-level questions spanning mathematics, science, and humanities,
measuring the agent’s capacity for deep reasoning at the boundary of
human expert knowledge. (iii) <span id="S5.p2.1.9"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Self-Evolving Code Agent
Benchmark.</span> Existing code benchmarks evaluate one-shot correctness
under fixed model capability and therefore cannot measure an agentś
self-evolution capability during inference. To directly assess this
self-evolution capability, we construct an in-house
<span id="S5.p2.1.10" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">LeetCode</span>
benchmark of 100 recently released problems across diverse algorithmic
categories (e.g., arrays, trees, linked lists), with reduced data
contamination. The agent solves each problem in multiple languages
(Python, C++, Java, Go, Kotlin), and we report acceptance rate,
test-case pass rate, runtime efficiency, and human-relative performance
metrics.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.1 </span>Experiments on Scientific and Mathematical Benchmarks

<div id="S5.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Experiment
Setting</span>. We evaluate <span id="S5.SS1.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGS</span> on
GPQA-Diamond, AIME24, and AIME25, focusing on evolving prompts and agent
outputs (problem solutions). Since these benchmarks primarily test
reasoning capability rather than tool use, we exclude external tools and
conduct a controlled comparison across three evolution strategies:
*Prompt-Evo*, *Solution-Evo*, and *Prompt-Solution-Joint-Evo*. We
evaluate across diverse mainstream language models using the reflection
optimizer with up to 3 rounds, after which the final agent output is
taken as the solution. Performance is measured by exact-match accuracy,
requiring the selected option to match the ground-truth answer for
GPQA-Diamond, and numerical output to exactly match the reference
integer for AIME24 and AIME25.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Results and
Analysis</span>. <a href="#S5.T2" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 5 Empirical Studies ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span>˜<span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> reveals four key observations.
(i) <span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Self-evolution yields consistent gains,
with greater benefit for weaker models.</span> Weaker models gain
substantially: <span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">gpt-4.1</span> improves by 71.4% on
AIME24 and 66.7% on AIME25 under PS-Joint-Evo, and
<span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">claude-sonnet-4.5</span> gains
13.0% and 22.7% respectively. Stronger models also benefit, albeit more
modestly: <span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">gemini-3-flash-preview</span>
(vanilla 88.4% GPQA-Diamond, 83.3% AIME24/25) improves by 2.3%, 12.0%,
and 12.0%, consistent with diminishing headroom at higher baselines.
(ii) <span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">PS-Joint-Evo consistently outperforms
single-strategy evolution.</span> For <span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.7"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">gpt-4.1</span> on AIME24,
Prompt-Evo reaches 33.3% and Solution-Evo 36.7%, while PS-Joint-Evo
reaches 40.0%, confirming that prompt and solution refinement address
complementary failure modes. (iii) <span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.8"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Math benchmarks benefit more than science
QA.</span> AIME24/25 show larger relative gains than GPQA-Diamond across
all models: for <span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.9"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">gpt-4.1</span>, AIME24 improves by
71.4% versus 3.9% on GPQA-Diamond. Long-horizon symbolic reasoning
exposes more intermediate failure points amenable to reflection, whereas
closed-book science QA relies more on factual recall. (iv)
<span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.10" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Ceiling effects
limit gains on saturated benchmarks.</span> <span id="S5.SS1.p2.1.11"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">grok-4.1-fast</span> reaches 96.7%
on AIME24 under vanilla, leaving negligible headroom and yielding no
gain from evolution. On GPQA-Diamond and AIME25 where its baselines are
lower (83.3% and 90.0%), it still improves by 7.2% and 7.4%, confirming
that self-evolution is most effective when sufficient headroom exists.
Overall, PS-Joint-Evo is the preferred strategy when inference budget
permits, as it addresses complementary failure modes simultaneously. For
cost-constrained deployment, evolution budgets are best allocated to
weaker models, harder tasks, or low-confidence samples. In
near-saturated settings, adaptive triggering based on confidence or task
difficulty is more effective than fixed-budget evolution.

</div>

</div>

self-evolution.</span></figcaption>
</figure>
<figure id="S5.SS2.54.54" class="ltx_figure ltx_minipage ltx_align_top"
style="width:345.0pt;">
<div id="S5.SS2.54.54.1" class="ltx_block">

class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="561"

<figure id="S5.F3" class="ltx_figure ltx_align_center">

<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span id="S5.F3.3.1.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure 3</span>: </span><span
id="S5.F3.4.2" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Performance
comparison of evolving and vanilla <span id="S5.F3.4.2.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGS</span>
within-inference.</span></figcaption>
</figure>
</div>
</figure>
</figure>

<div id="S5.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS2.p1.3.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Experiment
Setting</span>. For both GAIA and HLE, we focus on evolving agents
(including agent prompts, tool implementations, and agent code), as
these benchmarks primarily demand tool-augmented multi-step reasoning
rather than pure deductive inference. Our system deploys a top-level
planning agent ($`m=30`$) coordinating four specialized sub-agents (deep
researcher, browser-use, deep analyzer, tool calling agent and vibe
coding agent, each with $`m=20`$ using <span id="S5.SS2.p1.3.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">gemini-3.1-pro-preview</span>),
where $`m`$ denotes the maximum reasoning steps. Agent self-evolution is
driven by the vibe coding agent, which iteratively refines agent prompts
and code through the SEPL reflection optimizer, with evolved agents
registered as versioned RSPL resources and reused across subsequent
tasks. For GAIA, we report Pass@1 accuracy at each difficulty tier
(Level 1–3) and the overall average on both validation and test splits.
For HLE, we follow the official evaluation protocol using
<span id="S5.SS2.p1.3.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">o3-mini</span> as the judge.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Results and
Analysis</span>. <a href="#S5.T2" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 5 Empirical Studies ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span>˜<span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> and
<a href="#S5.F2" class="ltx_ref"
title="In Table 2 ‣ 5 Empirical Studies ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span>˜<span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> reveal three key observations.
(i) <span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGS<span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.2.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_upright"> achieves highly competitive
performance among systems with comparable backbone models.</span></span>
On GAIA, <span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGS</span> attains
strong results on both Test and Validation, reaching 89.04% on Test and
the best reported Validation score of 93.33% among all listed systems.
Although openJiuwen achieves a higher GAIA Test score, it relies on
substantially stronger backbone models, which are orthogonal to the
evolution protocol itself. On HLE, <span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGS</span> ranks
second overall, outperforming all systems except Claude Mythos Preview,
which similarly benefits from a more capable frontier backbone. These
comparisons suggest that the remaining gaps are primarily associated
with backbone strength rather than the proposed self-evolution protocol.
(ii) <span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.5" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Agent
evolution yields the largest gains on hard tasks.</span> On GAIA Test,
Agent-Evo improves the vanilla baseline by 12.6% on average, with gains
increasing with task difficulty. Improvements range from 8.2% on Level 1
to 33.3% on Level 3, indicating that harder tasks expose more
correctable failure modes for iterative refinement, while easier tasks
leave less room for improvement. This trend mirrors the headroom pattern
observed in the math benchmarks. (iii) <span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Self-evolution generalizes to open-ended
agent tasks.</span> GAIA requires coherent state management across
multi-domain transitions, such as from browser retrieval to file
analysis, while HLE demands expert-level multi-step reasoning. By
registering prompts, agent code, and tools as versioned RSPL resources,
<span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.7"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGS</span> preserves
task-critical state and reuses evolved capabilities across subsequent
subtasks. Overall, these results show that <span id="S5.SS2.p2.1.8"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span>’s
self-evolution protocol improves difficult agent tasks, remains
competitive among systems with comparable backbone models, and extends
from closed-form reasoning to complex, tool-intensive agent scenarios.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">5.3 </span>Experiments on Self-Evolving Code Agent Benchmark

<div id="S5.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Experiment
Setting</span>. Existing code generation benchmarks evaluate one-shot
generation and do not measure an agent’s ability to iteratively improve
solutions during inference. To address this, we construct a benchmark
based on the LeetCode online judge using 100 recently released problems
to mitigate contamination (details in <a href="#A4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Appendix D Details of the Self-Evolving Code Agent Benchmark ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Appendix</span>˜<span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">D</span></a>). We compare a vanilla
baseline against <span id="S5.SS3.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGS</span> with
Solution-Evo enabled across five languages (Python3, C++, Java, Go,
Kotlin), using <span id="S5.SS3.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">gemini-3-flash-preview</span> as
the backbone and a reflection budget of 3 rounds. We report three groups
of metrics covering functional correctness, runtime and memory
efficiency, and human-referenced competitiveness, with full definitions
provided in <a href="#A4.T6" class="ltx_ref"
title="In D.3 Evaluation Metrics ‣ Appendix D Details of the Self-Evolving Code Agent Benchmark ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span>˜<span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">6</span></a> in the appendix.

</div>

<div id="S5.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="S5.SS3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Results and
Analysis</span>. <a href="#S5.T3" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 5.2 Experiments on General Agent Benchmarks ‣ 5 Empirical Studies ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Table</span>˜<span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> and
<a href="#S5.F3" class="ltx_ref"
title="In 5.2 Experiments on General Agent Benchmarks ‣ 5 Empirical Studies ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Figure</span>˜<span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> reveal three key findings for
self-evolving code agents under execution-guided evaluation and
human-submission comparison. (i) <span id="S5.SS3.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Self-evolution consistently improves
functional correctness across languages.</span> Solution-Evo increases
pass rates by 10.1–26.7% across the five languages, with the largest
gain in Kotlin and high solved counts in compiled languages, including
99 problems in C++ and 98 in Java. Execution-blocking errors, including
compile, runtime, and answer errors, are reduced to near zero,
suggesting that inference-time feedback effectively repairs both format-
and logic-level failures. (ii) <span id="S5.SS3.p2.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Execution-guided evolution improves
efficiency beyond correctness.</span> Average runtime decreases in all
languages, with a 7.8% reduction in Python3 and larger reductions of
19.8–46.4% in compiled languages. These gains align with fewer
time-limit-exceeded errors, indicating that the agent not only fixes
invalid outputs but also discovers more efficient algorithms. Memory
usage decreases in most compiled languages by 4.8–16.6%, while
increasing modestly in Python3 and Kotlin, likely due to auxiliary data
structures introduced for correctness or speed. (iii)
<span id="S5.SS3.p2.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Evolved
solutions become more competitive relative to human submissions.</span>
Runtime beats improve in compiled languages by up to 30.8%, while
Python3 shows a modest decrease, consistent with a memory-speed
trade-off. Memory beats improve in four of the five languages by
7.0–23.5%, but decrease in Kotlin, suggesting that long-tail languages
may favor correctness over memory efficiency. Overall, Solution-Evo
provides a strong default strategy for algorithmic coding tasks by
combining execution feedback, iterative self-repair, and measurable
competitiveness against human submissions.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="S6" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">6 </span>Limitations and Impact Statement

<div id="S6.p1" class="ltx_para">

First, self-evolution introduces additional inference rounds that
increase latency and token consumption, and systematic analysis of the
efficiency-effectiveness trade-off under strict budget constraints
remains future work. Second, while <span id="S6.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> provides a
unified protocol interface for all RSPL resource types, our experiments
focus on Prompt-Evo, Solution-Evo, and Agent-Evo as primary comparison
targets. Evolution of Environment and Memory resources has been
implemented but not yet evaluated as independent ablation targets, and
we leave this to future work. On the impact side, self-evolving agent
systems may exhibit unintended behavioral drift if evolution objectives
are misspecified or reward signals are noisy. The version control and
rollback mechanisms in SEPL provide basic safeguards, but rigorous
alignment verification remains an open challenge for broader deployment.

</div>

</div>

<div id="S7" class="section ltx_section">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_section">7 </span>Conclusion

<div id="S7.p1" class="ltx_para">

We presented <span id="S7.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span>, a
two-layer self-evolution protocol that decouples the evolutionary
substrate from optimization logic, standardizing how agent resources are
registered, versioned, and evolved. Instantiated as <span id="S7.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGS</span>, the
protocol drives consistent improvements across scientific reasoning,
open-ended agent tasks, and algorithmic code generation, demonstrating
that a single evolution mechanism generalizes across task types and
resource categories. We believe <span id="S7.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> offers a
reusable foundation for future work on multi-agent collaboration, safe
online adaptation, and human-aligned self-improvement in dynamic
real-world environments.

</div>

<div class="ltx_pagination ltx_role_newpage">

</div>

<div id="A1" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix A </span>Notation

<div id="A1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We summarize the main mathematical symbols and their meanings in
Table <a href="#A1.T4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 4 ‣ Appendix A Notation ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a>. Symbols are organized into
six functional categories (highlighted in grey) following the two-layer
structure of <span id="A1.p1.10.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span>. The first
three cover the <span id="A1.p1.10.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">RSPL substrate</span>: indexing
conventions, the resource entity tuple, and protocol-registered
resources including the context manager $`\mathcal{M}_{\tau}`$ and
server interface $`\mathcal{A}_{\tau}`$. The remaining three cover the
<span id="A1.p1.10.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">SEPL layer</span>:
evolvable variables and the trainable subspace $`\Theta`$, the auxiliary
spaces ($`\mathcal{P}`$, $`\mathcal{Z}`$, $`\mathcal{H}`$,
$`\mathcal{D}`$, $`\mathcal{G}`$, $`\mathcal{S}`$) and five canonical
reflection operators $`\{\rho,\sigma,\iota,\varepsilon,\kappa\}`$, and
iteration-level variables of the evolutionary loop.

</div>

<div id="A2" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix B </span>Code, Prompts, and Resources

<div id="A2.p1" class="ltx_para">

All source code, agent prompts, and optimizer prompts for
<span id="A2.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> are
organized as follows.

</div>

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px1" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Agent and Optimizer Prompts.

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1" class="ltx_para">

All system prompts and task-specific prompts used by the agents, as well
as the prompts used by each optimizer instantiation (Reflection
Optimizer, TextGrad, Reinforce++, and GRPO), are provided in the
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">autogenesis/</span> directory of
the supplementary material. The directory is structured by component:
each subdirectory corresponds to a distinct agent role or optimizer
module and contains the associated prompt templates.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px2" class="section ltx_paragraph">

##### Self-Evolving Code Agent Benchmark Data.

<div id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1" class="ltx_para">

The benchmark problems, test cases, and reference solutions for the
Self-Evolving Code Agent Benchmark are provided in the
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">data/</span> directory of the
supplementary material. The dataset covers all collected
LeetCode-derived problems across five programming languages (Python,
C++, Java, JavaScript, and Go). The evaluation scripts are located in
<span id="A2.SS0.SSS0.Px2.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">autogenesis/src/benchmark/</span>.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix C </span>Comparison with Other Protocols

<div id="A3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Table <a href="#A3.T5" class="ltx_ref"
title="Table 5 ‣ Appendix C Comparison with Other Protocols ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a> provides a structured
protocol-level comparison between <span id="A3.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span>, Google
A2A \[<a href="#bib.bib12" class="ltx_ref"
title="A2A: a new era of agent interoperability">9</a>\], and Anthropic
MCP \[<a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Equipping agents for the real world with agent skills">1</a>\].
While A2A and MCP have standardized inter-agent communication and
model-to-tool invocation respectively, both operate solely at the level
of message passing and invocation, leaving internal resource states
opaque and providing no primitives for lifecycle management, version
lineage, or controlled state mutation. These are precisely the three
properties (<span id="A3.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Decoupling</span>, <span id="A3.p1.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Safety & Auditability</span>, and
<span id="A3.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Formalism</span>)
that <span id="A3.p1.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> is designed
to provide. The comparison is organized into five dimensions (grey
rows), with blue-highlighted entries marking capabilities that are
prerequisite for closed-loop self-evolution but absent from
communication- or invocation-centric protocols.

</div>
