<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table"><span id="A3.T5.51.4.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Table 5</span>: </span><span
id="A3.T5.6.3" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Protocol-level
comparison: <span id="A3.T5.6.3.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Autogenesis Protocol (AGP)</span> vs.
Google A2A vs. Anthropic MCP across key dimensions for agentic systems
and self-evolution. Symbols: <span class="math inline">✓</span> =
Supported, <span class="math inline">△</span> = Partial, <span
class="math inline">×</span> = Not supported. Highlighted rows (blue
background) emphasize evolution-enabling
capabilities.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A3.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">C.1 </span>Basic Information

<div id="A3.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A3.SS1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Proposer.</span>
Google’s A2A \[<a href="#bib.bib12" class="ltx_ref"
title="A2A: a new era of agent interoperability">9</a>\] is introduced
as a protocol for multi-agent communication, enabling agents to
collaborate through standardized interaction primitives. Anthropic’s
MCP \[<a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Equipping agents for the real world with agent skills">1</a>\]
standardizes model to tool invocation interfaces. In contrast,
<span id="A3.SS1.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> is proposed
in this work as a self-evolution protocol for composable, auditable, and
safely updateable agentic systems.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A3.SS1.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Protocol
Focus.</span> <span id="A3.SS1.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> focuses on
closed-loop improvement of agentic systems by organizing resource
updates through typed protocol operators and versioned state
transitions. A2A primarily addresses inter-agent communication and task
delegation. MCP primarily addresses standardized model to tool
invocation.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A3.SS1.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Entity
Scope.</span> <span id="A3.SS1.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> governs
heterogeneous entities, including prompts, agents, tools, environments,
and memory, as protocol-registered resources with explicit state and
version lineage. This design supports component-level evolution,
including prompt refinement and tool code updates. A2A treats agents and
tools as interaction endpoints without unified lifecycle management. MCP
exposes tools as callable interfaces but does not model them as
evolvable components with version lineage.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">C.2 </span>Agent and System Capabilities

<div id="A3.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A3.SS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Agent
First-Class.</span> <span id="A3.SS2.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> models
agents as managed protocol components with explicit schemas, metadata,
and lifecycle hooks. This enables registration, discovery,
orchestration, and controlled updates. A2A is agent-centric but treats
agents primarily as service endpoints without unified lifecycle
management or version lineage. MCP does not define agents as protocol
components and instead focuses on model to tool connectivity.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A3.SS2.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Multi-Agent.</span>
<span id="A3.SS2.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> supports
multi-agent configurations as part of its system substrate, enabling
coordinated execution with traceability and evolution-ready state. A2A
directly supports agent-to-agent collaboration. MCP does not treat
multi-agent orchestration as a protocol-level concern.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A3.SS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Execution
Tracing.</span> <span id="A3.SS2.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> provides
protocol-level trace capture over inputs, outputs, intermediate
decisions, and tool calls. These traces provide the learning signals
required for auditable evolution. A2A and MCP leave tracing to
application-level instrumentation, which can lead to inconsistent
observability across deployments.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A3.SS2.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Memory as
Resource.</span> <span id="A3.SS2.p4.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> models
memory as a first-class protocol resource with explicit read and write
interfaces, state, and version lineage. This enables persistent
cross-task improvement and reproducible evolution. A2A and MCP do not
prescribe a memory management protocol and instead delegate persistence
to external systems.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">C.3 </span>Evolvable Resource Management

<div id="A3.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A3.SS3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Lifecycle
Ops.</span> <span id="A3.SS3.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> provides
standardized lifecycle operators for initialization, registration,
construction, and decommissioning. These operators ensure that updates
are applied to well-defined and protocol-governed targets. A2A offers
partial lifecycle support for agents. MCP does not define lifecycle
management across heterogeneous component types.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A3.SS3.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Versioning and
Rollback.</span> Version lineage and rollback form the safety foundation
of closed-loop evolution. Each update produces an immutable snapshot
that supports comparison, auditing, and restoration after regressions.
<span id="A3.SS3.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> integrates
versioning as a first-class protocol capability. A2A and MCP do not
natively support version lineage over protocol-managed components, which
limits systematic evolution.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS3.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A3.SS3.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Registry and
Retrieval.</span> <span id="A3.SS3.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> maintains a
unified registry of protocol-registered resources and supports semantic
retrieval to reduce duplication and improve composability across tasks.
A2A and MCP provide partial discovery mechanisms, but they do not define
a unified management plane over heterogeneous component types.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS3.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A3.SS3.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Contract
Generation.</span> <span id="A3.SS3.p4.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> supports
automated generation of consolidated capability specifications that
enumerate tool actions, arguments, preconditions, and usage constraints.
This provides a principled form of context engineering that reduces
prompt bloat and improves orchestration reliability. A2A and MCP rely on
static descriptions or application-layer documentation without
protocol-level contract aggregation.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS4" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">C.4 </span>Self-Evolution Mechanism

<div id="A3.SS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A3.SS4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Closed-Loop
Evolution.</span> <span id="A3.SS4.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> is built
around an iterative improvement loop consisting of execution,
reflection, proposal generation, evaluation, and commitment. This loop
enables sustained and evidence-grounded refinement rather than one-off
adaptation. A2A and MCP do not provide native self-evolution primitives.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS4.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A3.SS4.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Operatorized
Updates.</span> <span id="A3.SS4.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> expresses
state mutations as typed and composable SEPL operators with well-defined
input and output contracts. This enables controlled and repeatable
evolution. A2A and MCP do not define a composable operator interface for
resource modification, leaving updates to application-specific logic.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS4.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A3.SS4.p3.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Auditability.</span>
<span id="A3.SS4.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> enforces
auditability at the protocol level by recording each state transition,
the execution evidence that motivated it, and the evaluation outcome
that justified it. This audit trail is supported by version lineage and
rollback. A2A and MCP provide only partial audit trails through external
instrumentation and do not offer protocol-level guarantees.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A3.SS5" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">C.5 </span>General and Ecosystem

<div id="A3.SS5.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A3.SS5.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Model-Agnostic.</span> This dimension
assesses whether a protocol can operate across different LLM backends
and providers. <span id="A3.SS5.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> is
model-agnostic by design through a unified model interface layer. A2A
and MCP are also broadly model-agnostic because they define interaction
standards rather than binding the protocol to a specific model.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS5.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A3.SS5.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Scalability.</span> Scalability
characterizes how coordination and discovery behave as the number of
components increases. <span id="A3.SS5.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> supports
scalable management by treating heterogeneous components as
registry-governed resources with retrieval mechanisms, enabling
efficient lookup and controlled orchestration. A2A may incur increasing
coordination overhead as interactions become denser in large multi-agent
settings. MCP standardizes tool interfaces but may still require
application-level orchestration for large tool or resource sets.

</div>

<div id="A3.SS5.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A3.SS5.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Open
Ecosystem.</span> Open ecosystem support refers to whether a protocol
can enable reusable and interoperable components.
<span id="A3.SS5.p3.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> provides a
protocol stack for managing, evolving, and auditing agentic components,
which supports component sharing and safe integration. A2A and MCP
provide partial ecosystem support through interoperability and tool
interface standardization, but they typically require additional layers
for evolution-ready resource management.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A4" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix D </span>Details of the Self-Evolving Code Agent Benchmark

<div id="A4.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">D.1 </span>Benchmark Design Rationale

<div id="A4.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Our benchmark is designed to evaluate self-evolving code agents under
execution-grounded and human-referenced conditions. Unlike conventional
code generation benchmarks that primarily assess final correctness,
self-evolving agents can improve within a single inference episode by
producing an initial solution, observing execution feedback, reflecting
on failure modes, and revising the solution accordingly. This adaptive
process requires a benchmark that measures not only whether the final
submission is accepted, but also how performance evolves throughout
refinement. Accordingly, our benchmark is motivated by three objectives:
(i) evaluating inference-time self-evolution on executable code, (ii)
calibrating agent performance against human submission distributions,
and (iii) assessing cross-language robustness under long-tail language
usage.

</div>

<div id="A4.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

The first objective is to make self-evolution directly measurable during
inference. In algorithmic coding tasks, execution feedback provides
concrete and fine-grained signals, including compilation status, runtime
errors, wrong answers, time-limit violations, memory-limit violations,
and execution statistics for accepted submissions. These signals allow
the agent to identify whether a failure stems from syntax errors,
interface mismatches, corner-case logic, algorithmic inefficiency, or
excessive resource usage. A benchmark for self-evolving agents should
therefore expose such feedback at each refinement round and record the
resulting improvement trajectory. This design distinguishes agents that
solve problems through stable and efficient refinement from those that
achieve correctness only through costly or unstable trial-and-error
behavior.

</div>

<div id="A4.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

The second objective is to evaluate coding performance relative to human
submissions. Absolute pass rates are informative but insufficient for
assessing practical coding competence, since they do not indicate
whether an accepted solution is efficient compared with human-written
solutions. We therefore build on the LeetCode online judge, which
reports runtime and memory usage for accepted submissions, together with
percentile-based *runtime beats* and *memory beats* statistics computed
from human submission distributions. These human-referenced metrics
provide an interpretable basis for assessing whether self-evolution
improves not only correctness, but also competitiveness relative to
human programmers.

</div>

<div id="A4.SS1.p4" class="ltx_para">

The third objective is to evaluate robustness across programming
languages, including long-tail languages. Many coding benchmarks are
dominated by Python or other high-resource languages, which can obscure
language-specific failures related to syntax, libraries, typing
discipline, compilation, and runtime behavior. LeetCode provides
standardized starter code across a broad set of languages, enabling the
same problem to be evaluated under comparable interfaces in Python3,
C++, Java, Go, and Kotlin. This design supports systematic analysis of
whether self-evolution generalizes across languages and whether
feedback-driven refinement remains effective across both high-resource
and lower-resource programming ecosystems.

</div>

<div id="A4.SS1.p5" class="ltx_para">

Overall, the benchmark provides a controlled setting for evaluating
self-evolving code agents as dynamic problem solvers. By combining
execution-based judging, iterative feedback, human-referenced efficiency
statistics, and multi-language evaluation, it jointly measures
functional correctness, resource efficiency, refinement dynamics, and
human-relative competitiveness under a unified protocol.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A4.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">D.2 </span>Benchmark Construction

<div id="A4.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A4.SS2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Data
Collection.</span> We collect the full set of 3,822 programming problems
available on LeetCode at the time of crawling. For each problem, we
extract the natural-language statement, official input and output
examples, constraints, platform-provided difficulty label, topical tags,
and language-specific starter code templates. The topical tags
characterize the algorithmic concepts required by each problem,
including arrays, trees, graphs, dynamic programming, greedy methods,
binary search, and mathematics. These annotations support stratified
analysis across difficulty levels, algorithmic categories, and
programming languages. Figure <a href="#A4.F5" class="ltx_ref"
title="Figure 5 ‣ D.2 Benchmark Construction ‣ Appendix D Details of the Self-Evolving Code Agent Benchmark ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">5</span></a> summarizes the tag and
difficulty distributions of the selected problems.

</div>

<figure id="A4.F5" class="ltx_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_figure">
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<figure id="A4.F5.1"
class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_minipage ltx_align_center ltx_align_top"
style="width:231.1pt;">
<img src="2604.15034v5/x4.png" id="A4.F5.1.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_landscape" width="830"
height="369" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span
id="A4.F5.1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure
4</span>: </span><span id="A4.F5.1.2.2" class="ltx_text"
style="font-size:90%;">Self-evolving code agent benchmark evaluation
pipeline.</span></figcaption>
</figure>
</div>
<div class="ltx_flex_cell ltx_flex_size_2">
<figure id="A4.F5.2"
class="ltx_figure ltx_figure_panel ltx_minipage ltx_align_center ltx_align_top"
style="width:106.9pt;">
<img src="2604.15034v5/x5.png" id="A4.F5.2.g1"
class="ltx_graphics ltx_centering ltx_img_square" width="830"
height="830" alt="Refer to caption" />
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_figure"><span
id="A4.F5.2.1.1.1" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:90%;">Figure
5</span>: </span><span id="A4.F5.2.2.2" class="ltx_text"
style="font-size:90%;">Problem distribution.</span></figcaption>
</figure>
</div>
</div>
</figure>

<div id="A4.SS2.p2" class="ltx_para">

The collected data are normalized into a unified problem representation.
Each instance contains a fixed task specification, official examples, a
language-specific starter template, and metadata for difficulty and
topic categories. We preserve the original interface required by the
online judge so that generated code can be submitted without modifying
function signatures or class definitions. This design ensures that
performance differences arise from agent behavior rather than
inconsistencies in task formatting or evaluation interfaces. We conduct
quality checks by filtering malformed records, removing duplicates, and
verifying that starter templates are available for all target languages.
From the full pool, we select 100 recently released problems to mitigate
training-data contamination. The selected problems span diverse topical
categories and difficulty levels, and are instantiated across Python3,
C++, Java, Go, and Kotlin to enable controlled cross-language
evaluation.

</div>

<div id="A4.SS2.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A4.SS2.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Problem
Characteristics.</span> LeetCode-style algorithmic problems provide a
controlled and challenging setting for evaluating code-agent competence.
Each task specifies explicit constraints and precise input and output
behavior, requiring instruction following, edge-case coverage, and
faithful implementation under a fixed interface. The breadth of tags and
difficulty levels evaluates algorithm selection, data-structure
proficiency, and complexity-aware reasoning. Because evaluation is
execution-based, brittle solutions can be exposed through concrete
failures such as off-by-one errors, corner-case bugs, interface
mismatches, and language-specific pitfalls. Standardized starter
templates across languages further enable systematic cross-language
comparison, including robustness analysis in long-tail languages. Since
a solution can be revised within the same inference episode, this
setting directly measures agentic capabilities such as self-repair,
feedback-grounded hypothesis testing, and efficiency-aware optimization
under runtime and memory constraints.

</div>

<div id="A4.SS2.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A4.SS2.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Problem
Evaluation.</span> For each problem, the agent receives a fixed input
representation and submits generated code to the official
execution-based judge, which evaluates functional correctness on hidden
test cases and reports resource usage statistics. This protocol ensures
all agents are assessed under identical task inputs, execution
conditions, and scoring criteria. When evaluating agents with
self-evolution capability, the agent is additionally allowed to
iteratively refine its solution within the same problem-solving episode
under a fixed budget of 3 rounds, using execution feedback from the
online judge to reflect on failure causes, identify actionable error
patterns, and propose targeted code revisions, while keeping the task
specification, prompt schema, and evaluation interface unchanged.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A4.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">D.3 </span>Evaluation Metrics

class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top"> <span id="A4.T6.6.7.1.1.1"
class="ltx_p" style="width:34.1pt;"><span id="A4.T6.6.7.1.1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:80%;">RE</span></span>
</span></td>
<td id="A4.T6.6.7.2" class="ltx_td ltx_align_justify ltx_align_top"

a runtime error during execution.</span></span> </span></td>

</span></td>

style="padding: -0.65pt 4.0pt"><span id="A4.T6.6.16.2.1"
class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top"> <span id="A4.T6.6.16.2.1.1"

class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">Percentage of accepted solutions
whose runtime outperforms human submissions.</span></span> </span></td>
</tr>
<tr id="A4.T6.6.17" class="ltx_tr">
<td id="A4.T6.6.17.1"
class="ltx_td ltx_align_justify ltx_align_top ltx_border_bb"
style="padding: -0.65pt 4.0pt"><span id="A4.T6.6.17.1.1"
class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top"> <span id="A4.T6.6.17.1.1.1"
class="ltx_p" style="width:34.1pt;"><span id="A4.T6.6.17.1.1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold" style="font-size:80%;">AMB</span></span>
</span></td>
<td id="A4.T6.6.17.2"
class="ltx_td ltx_align_justify ltx_align_top ltx_border_bb"
style="padding: -0.65pt 4.0pt"><span id="A4.T6.6.17.2.1"
class="ltx_inline-block ltx_align_top"> <span id="A4.T6.6.17.2.1.1"
class="ltx_p" style="width:256.1pt;"><span id="A4.T6.6.17.2.1.1.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">Percentage of accepted solutions
whose memory usage outperforms human submissions.</span></span>
</span></td>
</tr>
</tbody>
</table>
<figcaption><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_table"><span id="A4.T6.4.1.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:113%;">Table 6</span>: </span><span
id="A4.T6.5.2" class="ltx_text" style="font-size:113%;">Evaluation
metrics for the algorithmic coding benchmark.</span></figcaption>
</figure>

<div id="A4.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

We report three groups of metrics that capture complementary aspects of
code-agent performance. Capability metrics evaluate functional
correctness and diagnose execution-blocking failure modes. PR measures
the number of fully accepted problems, while TLE, MLE, CE, RE, WA, TO,
and RpE identify whether failures arise from algorithmic inefficiency,
excessive memory use, compilation errors, runtime exceptions, incorrect
logic, response timeout, or invalid output formatting. These metrics are
particularly important for self-evolving agents because different
failure modes correspond to different refinement opportunities.

</div>

<div id="A4.SS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

Efficiency metrics characterize the computational quality of accepted
solutions and the progress made by partially correct submissions. AR and
AM summarize runtime and memory usage over accepted solutions, which
allows us to assess whether self-evolution improves efficiency rather
than merely increasing pass rate. APC measures how many test cases are
passed before failure and provides a fine-grained signal for
unsuccessful submissions. This metric is useful when an agent progresses
from early failure to passing most hidden tests, even if the final
solution is not accepted.

</div>

<div id="A4.SS3.p3" class="ltx_para">

Human-referenced metrics situate accepted agent solutions within the
empirical distribution of human submissions. ARB measures the fraction
of accepted human submissions whose runtime is slower than the agent
solution, while AMB measures the analogous fraction for memory usage.
These metrics provide an interpretable basis for comparing evolved agent
solutions with human-written solutions and help determine whether an
accepted solution is merely correct or also competitive.

</div>

<div id="A4.SS3.p4" class="ltx_para">

For self-evolving agents, all metrics can be computed at each refinement
round as well as for the final submission. This enables trajectory-level
evaluation of inference-time improvement, including whether correctness
increases across rounds, whether runtime and memory usage improve or
degrade, and whether human-relative competitiveness changes after
reflection and revision. The benchmark therefore evaluates both endpoint
performance and the refinement process through which an agent reaches
that endpoint.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5" class="section ltx_appendix">

## <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_appendix">Appendix E </span>Details of Self-Evolution Protocol

<div id="A5.SS1" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">E.1 </span>Design Motivation

<div id="A5.SS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

Existing LLM-based agent systems pursue self-improvement in largely ad
hoc ways: prompts, tools, and memory are tightly coupled to agent logic,
updated without version control, and impossible to roll back when an
update degrades performance. This architecture fragility motivates the
two-layer design of <span id="A5.SS1.p1.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span>. We outline
the core motivating principles below.

</div>

<div id="A5.SS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

- <span id="A5.I1.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I1.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I1.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Decoupling
  substrate from logic.</span> In most existing frameworks, what an
  agent operates over (prompts, tools, memory) and how it evolves them
  (optimization algorithms, feedback loops) are interleaved in a single
  codebase. This coupling makes it difficult to swap, reuse, or safely
  update individual components. <span id="A5.I1.i1.p1.1.2"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> separates
  the *evolvable substrate* (RSPL) from the *evolution logic* (SEPL), so
  that any compliant optimizer can be applied to any registered resource
  without modifying the component itself. This modularity is essential
  for principled, reproducible self-evolution.

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I1.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I1.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I1.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Safety and
  auditability through lifecycle management.</span> Self-evolving agents
  modify their own components at runtime, which introduces risks of
  cascading failures or undetectable regressions. Without explicit
  version control and rollback support, a single bad update can silently
  degrade system behavior. RSPL endows every resource with versioned
  state and a controlled mutation interface, ensuring that each
  evolutionary step is traceable, reversible, and subject to explicit
  commit or rollback decisions. SEPL enforces that updates proceed only
  after formal evaluation, making every change auditable by design.

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I1.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I1.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I1.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Formalism
  over heuristics.</span> Prior self-evolution approaches apply
  modifications heuristically, for example, by prompting a model to
  "improve itself" or by directly patching code, with no standardized
  interface governing what constitutes a valid update cycle. This
  informality makes it impossible to guarantee safety or reason about
  correctness across runs. SEPL formalizes the update cycle as a
  closed-loop operator interface, transforming ad hoc modifications into
  a rigorous protocol with well-defined pre- and post-conditions. This
  formalism enables algorithm-agnostic instantiation: the same operator
  interface supports prompt optimization, reinforcement learning, and
  gradient-free search.

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I1.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I1.i4.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I1.i4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Uniform
  abstraction across heterogeneous components.</span> Agent systems
  compose heterogeneous entities, including LLM instructions, external
  tool scripts, MCP services, and in-context memory, that are typically
  managed through disparate, component-specific mechanisms.
  <span id="A5.I1.i4.p1.1.2"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold ltx_font_smallcaps">AGP</span> provides
  a single, unified resource entity abstraction that encompasses all
  five types (Prompt, Agent, Tool, Environment, Memory), enabling SEPL
  to apply the same evolution operators uniformly across all components
  without special-casing.

  </div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

Together, these principles ground the two-layer architecture in a
coherent design philosophy: RSPL provides a stable, typed, versioned
substrate that renders agent internals observable and controllable,
while SEPL provides a safe, formal operator interface that governs how
those internals are updated. The remainder of this section specifies
each layer in detail.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS2" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">E.2 </span>Layer 1: Resource Substrate Protocol Layer

<div id="A5.SS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

The Resource Substrate Protocol Layer (RSPL) defines the evolvable
substrate as a set of protocol-registered resources with explicit state,
lifecycle, and version lineage. In this paper, these resources comprise
(i) *instructions* (<span id="A5.SS2.p1.1.2"
class="ltx_text">Prompt</span>), (ii) *decision policies*
(<span id="A5.SS2.p1.1.4" class="ltx_text">Agent</span>), (iii)
*actuation interfaces* (<span id="A5.SS2.p1.1.6"
class="ltx_text">Tool</span>), which encompass native tool scripts, MCP
tools \[<a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Equipping agents for the real world with agent skills">1</a>\],
and agent skills \[<a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Introduction to agent skills">2</a>\], (iv) *task/world dynamics*
(<span id="A5.SS2.p1.1.8" class="ltx_text">Environment</span>), and (v)
*persistent state* (<span id="A5.SS2.p1.1.10"
class="ltx_text">Memory</span>). Crucially, resources in RSPL are
*passive*: they encapsulate no optimization logic and cannot
self-modify; all observations and state transitions occur only through
controlled, interface-mediated operations invoked by higher layers.

</div>

<div id="A5.SS2.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">E.2.1 </span>Core Entities

<div id="A5.SS2.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

We focus on these five entity types as a minimal yet expressive
substrate for agentic systems. This choice is not intended to be
exhaustive, but rather to identify a common denominator across modern
agent stacks and provide a uniform target space on which SEPL can
operate.

</div>

<div id="A5.Thmtheorem1" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_definition">

###### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="A5.Thmtheorem1.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Definition E.1</span></span><span id="A5.Thmtheorem1.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"> </span>(Resource Entity)<span id="A5.Thmtheorem1.3.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span>

<div id="A5.Thmtheorem1.p1" class="ltx_para">

A resource entity of type $`\tau`$ and its type-level collection can be
represented as:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle e_{\tau,i}`$ | $`\displaystyle=(n_{\tau,i},\,d_{\tau,i},\,\phi_{\tau,i},\,g_{\tau,i},\,m_{\tau,i}),`$ |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equationgroup ltx_align_right">(6)</span> |
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{\tau}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\{\,e_{\tau,i}\mid i\in\mathcal{I}_{\tau}\,\},`$ |  |  |

where
$`\mathcal{T}=\{\textsc{Prompt},\textsc{Agent},\textsc{Tool},\textsc{Env},\textsc{Mem}\}`$
denotes the set of RSPL entity types, $`\tau\in\mathcal{T}`$ indexes the
entity type, $`\mathcal{I}_{\tau}`$ is the index set of resource
instances of type $`\tau`$, and $`i\in\mathcal{I}_{\tau}`$ indexes an
individual instance. Here $`n_{\tau,i}`$ is a unique resource name,
$`d_{\tau,i}`$ is a short description,
$`\phi_{\tau,i}:\mathcal{X}_{\tau}\rightarrow\mathcal{Y}_{\tau}`$ is an
input-to-output mapping, $`g_{\tau,i}\in\{0,1\}`$ is an evolvability
marker, and $`m_{\tau,i}`$ is an auxiliary metadata dictionary.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS2.SSS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

A key motivation for making prompt, tool, and memory explicit RSPL
resources is *decoupling*. Many agent systems package prompts, tools,
and memory as internal components of an agent, which entangles agent
logic with task-specific instructions and capability bundles, increasing
maintenance and limiting transfer. By externalizing them as first-class,
versioned resources with standardized interfaces, the same tool-calling
agent policy can be paired with different prompts and tool sets, and
deployed unchanged across tasks and environments.

</div>

<div id="A5.SS2.SSS1.p3" class="ltx_para">

To support resource registration, unified management, and instantiation,
RSPL stores a serializable registration record for each resource
instance.

</div>

<div id="A5.Thmtheorem2" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_definition">

###### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="A5.Thmtheorem2.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Definition E.2</span></span><span id="A5.Thmtheorem2.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"> </span>(Resource Registration Record)<span id="A5.Thmtheorem2.3.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span>

<div id="A5.Thmtheorem2.p1" class="ltx_para">

A resource registration record and its type-level collection can be
represented as:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | $`\displaystyle c_{\tau,i}`$ | $`\displaystyle=(e_{\tau,i},\,v_{\tau,i},\,\eta_{\tau,i},\,\theta_{\tau,i},\,\mathcal{F}_{\tau,i}),`$ |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equationgroup ltx_align_right">(7)</span> |
|  | $`\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{\tau}`$ | $`\displaystyle=\{\,c_{\tau,i}\mid i\in\mathcal{I}_{\tau}\,\},`$ |  |  |

where $`\tau\in\mathcal{T}`$ indexes the entity type and
$`i\in\mathcal{I}_{\tau}`$ indexes an individual instance. Here
$`e_{\tau,i}`$ is the resource entity tuple defined in
<a href="#A5.Thmtheorem1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Definition E.1 (Resource Entity). ‣ E.2.1 Core Entities ‣ E.2 Layer 1: Resource Substrate Protocol Layer ‣ Appendix E Details of Self-Evolution Protocol ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Definition</span>˜<span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">E.1</span></a>, $`v_{\tau,i}\in\mathbb{V}`$
is a version string, $`\eta_{\tau,i}`$ is an implementation descriptor
(e.g., import path, class definition, or source-code string),
$`\theta_{\tau,i}`$ are instantiation parameters (e.g., constructor
arguments), and $`\mathcal{F}_{\tau,i}`$ is a set of exported
representations used by LLMs to interact with the resource (e.g.,
function-calling schema, plain text, and structured argument schema).

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5.Thmtheorem3" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_definition">

###### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="A5.Thmtheorem3.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Definition E.3</span></span><span id="A5.Thmtheorem3.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"> </span>(Protocol-registered resource)<span id="A5.Thmtheorem3.3.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span>

<div id="A5.Thmtheorem3.p1" class="ltx_para">

For each entity type $`\tau`$, let $`\mathcal{R}_{\tau}`$ denote the
type-specific registry of protocol-registered resources, and let
$`\mathcal{R}=\bigcup_{\tau}\mathcal{R}_{\tau}`$ denote the global
registry. RSPL binds each entity type $`\tau`$ to a dedicated context
manager $`\mathcal{M}_{\tau}`$ and a server-exposed interface
$`\mathcal{A}_{\tau}`$. We represent the type-level registered resource
as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
r_{\tau}=(\mathcal{C}_{\tau},\;\mathcal{M}_{\tau},\;\mathcal{A}_{\tau}),
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(8)</span> |

where each $`c_{\tau,i}\in\mathcal{C}_{\tau}`$ is a registration record
in <a href="#A5.Thmtheorem2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Definition E.2 (Resource Registration Record). ‣ E.2.1 Core Entities ‣ E.2 Layer 1: Resource Substrate Protocol Layer ‣ Appendix E Details of Self-Evolution Protocol ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">Definition</span>˜<span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">E.2</span></a>. The context manager
$`\mathcal{M}_{\tau}`$ maintains the collection $`\mathcal{C}_{\tau}`$,
the version lineage for type $`\tau`$, and implements lifecycle and
update operations over these records; the server-exposed interface
$`\mathcal{A}_{\tau}`$ encapsulates $`\mathcal{M}_{\tau}`$ and exposes a
unified external interface by delegating requests to the corresponding
context-manager routines.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS2.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">E.2.2 </span>Context Manager

<div id="A5.SS2.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

The context manager implements the management plane for each resource
type. Beyond lifecycle control and dependency constraints, it maintains
(i) an active registry of materialized resources and (ii) a versioned
history for restoration. Its exported API exposes operators for
lifecycle (<span id="A5.SS2.SSS2.p1.2.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">init</span>,
<span id="A5.SS2.SSS2.p1.2.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">build</span>), retrieval
(<span id="A5.SS2.SSS2.p1.2.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">list</span>,
<span id="A5.SS2.SSS2.p1.2.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">get</span>), versioning
(<span id="A5.SS2.SSS2.p1.2.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">update</span>,
<span id="A5.SS2.SSS2.p1.2.6"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">restore</span>), execution
(<span id="A5.SS2.SSS2.p1.2.7"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">run</span>), and serialization
(<span id="A5.SS2.SSS2.p1.2.8"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">save_to_json</span>,
<span id="A5.SS2.SSS2.p1.2.9"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">load_from_json</span>,
<span id="A5.SS2.SSS2.p1.2.10"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">save_contract</span>,
<span id="A5.SS2.SSS2.p1.2.11"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">load_contract</span>). The manager
explicitly supports *contract generation*, producing a consolidated
capability and constraint specification for the managed entities, which
provides stable, up-to-date descriptions that improve reliability and
reduce prompt bloat, enabling systematic *context engineering* via
controlled prompt injection. For instance, for tools (which may be
native tool scripts, MCP-connected
tools \[<a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Equipping agents for the real world with agent skills">1</a>\],
or agent skills) the contract can take a <span id="A5.SS2.SSS2.p1.2.14"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">skills.md</span>-style
form \[<a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Introduction to agent skills">2</a>\] that enumerates tool
actions, arguments, preconditions, and usage constraints. The exported
management interface implemented by $`\mathcal{M}_{\tau}`$ and exposed
by $`\mathcal{A}_{\tau}`$ are as follows:

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS2.SSS3" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">E.2.3 </span>Server Interface

<div id="A5.SS2.SSS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

The server is introduced to encapsulate the context manager’s internal
complexity and present a stable, simplified interface for external
callers. It packages heterogeneous management routines behind a uniform
set of endpoints with consistent request/response semantics, while
delegating the implementation details to the context manager. This
separation isolates clients from internal design changes, reduces
coupling, and provides a single control plane through which the protocol
mediates safe, version-aware interactions with RSPL resources.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS2.SSS4" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">E.2.4 </span>Infrastructure Services

<div id="A5.SS2.SSS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

RSPL further includes cross-cutting services that support reliable
evolution, including reproducibility, safe deployment, and versioned
recovery:

</div>

<div id="A5.SS2.SSS4.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A5.SS2.SSS4.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Model
manager.</span> A unified model-API layer that standardizes calls across
providers (e.g., OpenAI, Anthropic, Google, and OpenRouter, etc.), while
supporting routing, fallback, and cost-aware selection to keep model
access consistent as components evolve.

</div>

<div id="A5.SS2.SSS4.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A5.SS2.SSS4.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Version
manager.</span> Maintains version lineage for each resource, enabling
rollback, branching, and diffing. Versions are auto-incremented
identifiers (e.g., semantic versions) assigned on register or update,
each referencing an immutable snapshot of the configuration record and
associated artifacts for auditability and reproducibility.

</div>

<div id="A5.SS2.SSS4.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A5.SS2.SSS4.p4.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Dynamic
manager.</span> Handles serialization and deserialization of resource
configurations for persistence and transfer, enabling safe hot-swapping
of resource configurations at runtime without restarting the agent
system.

</div>

<div id="A5.SS2.SSS4.p5" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A5.SS2.SSS4.p5.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Trace
manager.</span> Captures fine-grained execution traces (inputs, outputs,
intermediate decisions, tool interactions, etc.) for interpretability
and debugging, and as training signals for dataset synthesis and
retrospective improvement.

</div>

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS3" class="section ltx_subsection">

### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsection">E.3 </span>Layer 2: Self-Evolution Protocol Layer

<div id="A5.SS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

The Self-Evolution Protocol Layer (SEPL) formalizes agentic system
evolution as a generalized optimization problem over a heterogeneous
state space, modeling evolutionary dynamics as a state transition
function governed by a strictly typed operator algebra. By mediating all
state mutations through standardized RSPL interfaces, SEPL guarantees
that evolution is traceable, reversible, and safe-by-construction. While
this paper focuses on the reflection-driven optimizer as the primary
instantiation, the same state manipulation primitives also accommodate
textual-gradient methods such as
TextGrad \[<a href="#bib.bib9" class="ltx_ref"
title="Optimizing generative ai by backpropagating language model feedback">40</a>\]
and reinforcement learning approaches such as
GRPO \[<a href="#bib.bib8" class="ltx_ref"
title="Deepseekmath: pushing the limits of mathematical reasoning in open language models">31</a>\]
and Reinforce++ \[<a href="#bib.bib10" class="ltx_ref"
title="Reinforce++: a simple and efficient approach for aligning large language models">14</a>\].

</div>

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS1" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">E.3.1 </span>Evolvable Variables

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS1.p1" class="ltx_para">

To transition from heuristic adaptation to a systematic evolution
protocol, SEPL introduces the concept of *variable lifting*: projecting
discrete, heterogeneous RSPL resources (e.g., tool code, system prompts,
memory modules, and environment configurations) onto a unified
representation of evolvable variables. This homogenizes the interaction
surface for all evolutionary operators and rigorously delineates the
trainable subspace via an explicit learnability mask.

</div>

<div id="A5.Thmtheorem4" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_definition">

###### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="A5.Thmtheorem4.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Definition E.4</span></span><span id="A5.Thmtheorem4.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"> </span>(Evolvable Variable Set)<span id="A5.Thmtheorem4.3.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span>

<div id="A5.Thmtheorem4.p1" class="ltx_para">

We define the universal set of evolvable variables as

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}=\Bigl(\bigcup_{\tau\in\mathcal{T}}\mathcal{E}_{\tau}\Bigr)\cup\{y\},
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(9)</span> |

where $`\mathcal{E}_{\tau}`$ denotes the set of resource entities of
type $`\tau`$ governed by RSPL, and $`y`$ encapsulates execution
artifacts (final outputs and reasoning traces) that constitute the
observational basis for retrospective optimization. Each variable
$`v\in\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}`$ is associated with a binary
learnability constraint $`g_{v}\in\{0,1\}`$, strictly defining the
trainable parameter subspace

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\Theta=\{v\in\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}\mid g_{v}=1\}.
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(10)</span> |

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS1.p2" class="ltx_para">

The evolvability marker $`g_{v}`$ allows SEPL to operate selectively:
frozen components (e.g., a fixed tool API) are excluded from the
trainable subspace, while designated evolvable resources (e.g., system
prompts, tool implementations) are exposed for modification. This
explicit masking ensures that only intended components are mutated
during evolution.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS2" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">E.3.2 </span>Operator Algebra

<div id="A5.Thmtheorem5" class="ltx_theorem ltx_theorem_definition">

###### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_theorem"><span id="A5.Thmtheorem5.1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Definition E.5</span></span><span id="A5.Thmtheorem5.2.2" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold"> </span>(SEPL Operator)<span id="A5.Thmtheorem5.3.3" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">.</span>

<div id="A5.Thmtheorem5.p1" class="ltx_para">

Let $`\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}`$ be the evolvable variable set and
$`\mathcal{P}`$ a *message space* carrying auxiliary signals (e.g.,
traces, hypotheses, gradients, or reward signals) passed between
operators. A *SEPL operator* is a function

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
f:\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}\times\mathcal{P}_{\text{in}}\;\rightarrow\;\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\text{evo}}\times\mathcal{P}_{\text{out}},
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(11)</span> |

where
$`\mathcal{P}_{\text{in}},\mathcal{P}_{\text{out}}\subseteq\mathcal{P}`$
are the incoming and outgoing message types, and
$`\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\text{evo}}`$ is the updated evolvable state.
Operators are *composable*: the output
$`(\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\text{evo}},\mathcal{P}_{\text{out}})`$ of one
operator serves as the input to the next, enabling the construction of
an evolutionary pipeline $`f_{n}\circ\cdots\circ f_{1}`$. All mutations
to $`\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}`$ must be routed through RSPL interfaces,
ensuring every state transition is versioned, auditable, and reversible
regardless of the specific optimizer instantiation.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS2.p1" class="ltx_para">

The auxiliary spaces used by operators are: trace space $`\mathcal{Z}`$
(execution observations), hypothesis space $`\mathcal{H}`$ (causal
failure attributions), modification space $`\mathcal{D}`$ (proposed
resource changes), objective specification $`\mathcal{G}`$ (task goals
and safety invariants), and evaluation space $`\mathcal{S}`$
(performance metrics and safety status). The five canonical operators of
the reflection instantiation are
$`\{\rho,\sigma,\iota,\varepsilon,\kappa\}`$, corresponding to
<span id="A5.SS3.SSS2.p1.6.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Reflect</span>,
<span id="A5.SS3.SSS2.p1.6.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Select</span>,
<span id="A5.SS3.SSS2.p1.6.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Improve</span>,
<span id="A5.SS3.SSS2.p1.6.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Evaluate</span>, and
<span id="A5.SS3.SSS2.p1.6.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Commit</span>, operating over these
spaces in sequence. Other instantiations (TextGrad, GRPO, Reinforce++)
reuse the same operator interface but replace the internal logic of
individual operators, as detailed in the method-specific subsections
below.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS3" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">E.3.3 </span>Evolutionary Loop

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS3.p1" class="ltx_para">

Given an initial evolvable state $`\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}^{(0)}`$ and
an empty message $`\mathcal{P}^{(0)}=\emptyset`$, the evolutionary loop
at each iteration $`t`$ applies a sequence of operators
$`f_{1},\ldots,f_{n}`$ in composition:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | 
``` math
\bigl(\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}^{(t+1)},\,\mathcal{P}^{(t+1)}\bigr)=(f_{n}\circ\cdots\circ f_{1})\bigl(\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}^{(t)},\,\mathcal{P}^{(t)}\bigr),
``` |  | <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_equation ltx_align_right">(12)</span> |

where each $`f_{i}`$ reads the current state and incoming messages,
produces an updated state and outgoing messages consumed by $`f_{i+1}`$.
The loop repeats until convergence or budget exhaustion. By routing all
state mutations through RSPL interfaces, each transition is versioned
and reversible, guaranteeing that evolution is *grounded* in execution
data, *traceable* through versioned updates, and *safe-by-construction*.

</div>

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS3.p2" class="ltx_para">

The specific operator sequence instantiated by each method determines
the behavior of the loop. The reflection optimizer instantiates this
loop with five operators: <span id="A5.SS3.SSS3.p2.1.1"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Reflect</span> maps execution traces
and current state to causal failure hypotheses,
<span id="A5.SS3.SSS3.p2.1.2"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Select</span> identifies target
evolvable entities and generates concrete modification proposals,
<span id="A5.SS3.SSS3.p2.1.3"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Improve</span> applies proposals via
RSPL interfaces to yield a candidate state,
<span id="A5.SS3.SSS3.p2.1.4"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Evaluate</span> scores the candidate
against the objective and safety invariants, and
<span id="A5.SS3.SSS3.p2.1.5"
class="ltx_text ltx_font_smallcaps">Commit</span> conditionally accepts
or rolls back the transition. TextGrad, GRPO, and Reinforce++ reuse the
same loop structure but replace the internal logic of individual
operators, as detailed in the method-specific subsections below.

</div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS4" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">E.3.4 </span>Reflection Optimizer

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS4.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A5.SS3.SSS4.p1.2.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Evolvable
Variables.</span> In the reflection-driven instantiation, the evolvable
state is given by the lifted variable set $`\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}`$
introduced above. Concretely, $`\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}`$ includes
RSPL-managed resources (e.g., prompts, tools, memories, and agent
components) together with execution artifacts (e.g., the produced answer
and reasoning trace). A binary learnability mask specifies which
variables may be modified, allowing the optimizer to target only
authorized components while keeping non-learnable resources fixed.

</div>

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS4.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A5.SS3.SSS4.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Operator
Algebra.</span> We instantiate SEPL with the canonical reflection-driven
operator suite. The operator signatures and their intended roles are as
follows.

</div>

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS4.p3" class="ltx_para">

- <span id="A5.I2.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I2.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I2.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Reflect
  ($`\rho`$).</span> Defined as
  $`\rho:\mathcal{Z}\times\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}\rightarrow\wp(\mathcal{H})`$,
  this operator bridges the gap between raw observation and optimization
  direction. It approximates the “semantic gradient” of the system by
  mapping high-dimensional execution traces to specific, causal failure
  hypotheses within the variable space.

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I2.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I2.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I2.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Select
  ($`\sigma`$).</span> Formulated as
  $`\sigma:\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}\times\wp(\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\wp(\mathcal{D})`$,
  this operator acts as the targeting policy. It identifies which
  evolvable entities within $`\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}`$ are implicated
  by the diagnostic hypotheses, then generates concrete modification
  proposals $`\mathcal{D}`$ targeting those entities, subject to
  structural constraints.

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I2.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I2.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I2.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Improve
  ($`\iota`$).</span> The mutation operator,
  $`\iota:\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}\times\wp(\mathcal{D})\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\text{evo}}`$,
  executes the physical state transition. It applies discrete updates
  $`\mathcal{D}`$ via standardized RSPL interfaces to yield a
  provisional candidate state.

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I2.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I2.i4.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I2.i4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Evaluate
  ($`\varepsilon`$).</span> Specified as
  $`\varepsilon:\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\text{evo}}\times\mathcal{G}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}`$,
  this operator serves as the objective function. It maps the candidate
  state and goal specification to the evaluation space $`\mathcal{S}`$
  (comprising quantitative scores and strict safety invariants).

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I2.i5"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I2.i5.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I2.i5.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Commit
  ($`\kappa`$).</span> Operating as
  $`\kappa:\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\text{evo}}\times\mathcal{S}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}`$,
  this function acts as a conditional gating mechanism. It utilizes the
  evaluation signals in $`\mathcal{S}`$ to govern state transition,
  rigorously enforcing safety invariants and performance monotonicity by
  accepting the candidate $`\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\text{evo}}`$ only
  when specific success criteria are met.

  </div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS4.p4" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A5.SS3.SSS4.p4.13.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">The
Evolutionary Loop.</span> These operators are composed into the
reflection-driven closed-loop procedure shown in
Algorithm <a href="#alg1" class="ltx_ref"
title="Algorithm 1 ‣ E.3.4 Reflection Optimizer ‣ E.3 Layer 2: Self-Evolution Protocol Layer ‣ Appendix E Details of Self-Evolution Protocol ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">1</span></a>. Starting from an initial
lifted state $`\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}^{(0)}`$, the agent first
executes to collect an observational trace $`\mathcal{Z}`$ (tool
outputs, intermediate decisions, failures, and progress signals). The
reflect operator $`\rho`$ maps $`\mathcal{Z}`$ to a set of causal
hypotheses $`\mathcal{H}`$, which are then translated by $`\sigma`$ into
concrete modification primitives $`\mathcal{D}`$ (e.g., prompt edits,
tool adjustments, or memory updates) over the learnable subset of
$`\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}`$. The improve operator $`\iota`$ applies
$`\mathcal{D}`$ via RSPL interfaces to obtain a candidate state, which
is evaluated by $`\varepsilon`$ to produce $`\mathcal{S}`$ capturing
both performance metrics and safety constraints. Finally, the commit
operator $`\kappa`$ gates the transition by accepting only candidates
that satisfy the predefined criteria, recording each accepted change as
a versioned resource update with auditable lineage and enabling rollback
when necessary.

</div>

<figure id="alg1"
class="ltx_float ltx_float_algorithm ltx_framed ltx_framed_top">
<div id="alg1.3" class="ltx_listing">
<div id="alg1.l0" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg1.l0.1.1.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">0:</span></span> Agentic System
<span class="math inline">𝒜</span>, Objective <span
class="math inline">𝒢</span>, Budget <span
class="math inline"><em>T</em></span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l0a" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg1.l0a.1.1.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">0:</span></span> Optimized state
<span class="math inline">𝒱<sub>evo</sub><sup>*</sup></span>
</div>
<div id="alg1.l1" class="ltx_listingline">

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS5" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">E.3.5 </span>TextGrad Optimizer

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS5.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A5.SS3.SSS5.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Evolvable
Variables.</span> In the TextGrad instantiation, the evolvable variables
are restricted to a subset of *prompt variables* marked as optimizable
and lifted into TextGrad variables with explicit role descriptions. In
our implementation, each optimizable prompt module is represented as a
TextGrad variable whose value is the current prompt text and whose role
description specifies the prompt’s function, enabling the optimizer to
condition updates on its intended semantics.

</div>

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS5.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A5.SS3.SSS5.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Operator
Algebra.</span> TextGrad instantiates SEPL with a prompt-level
operatorization in which “gradients” are natural-language critiques
produced by an LLM evaluator and updates are implemented as constrained
prompt rewrites. Following the standard TextGrad view, we express the
method with five core operators, namely *Execute*, *Loss*, *Backward*,
*Improve*, and *Commit*, where the “gradient” is a piece of text (a
critique) rather than a numeric vector:

- <span id="A5.I3.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I3.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I3.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Execute
  ($`\chi_{\mathrm{tg}}`$).</span>
  $`\chi_{\mathrm{tg}}:(A,\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}},x,f)\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}`$
  runs the agent under the current prompt variables and produces an
  execution trace/outcome.

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I3.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I3.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I3.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Loss
  ($`\lambda_{\mathrm{tg}}`$).</span>
  $`\lambda_{\mathrm{tg}}:\mathcal{Z}\rightarrow\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{tg}}`$,
  where $`\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{tg}}`$ is a space of natural-language
  critiques (textual gradients). In our implementation,
  $`\lambda_{\mathrm{tg}}`$ is realized by <span id="A5.I3.i2.p1.4.2"
  class="ltx_text ltx_font_typewriter">TextLoss</span>, which queries an
  evaluator LLM and returns critique feedback.

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I3.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I3.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I3.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Backward
  ($`\beta_{\mathrm{tg}}`$).</span>
  $`\beta_{\mathrm{tg}}:\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}\times\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{tg}}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}`$
  assigns textual gradients to optimizable prompt variables by storing
  the critique (optionally with context) in a per-variable gradient
  buffer. In our current implementation, we distribute the same critique
  to each optimizable prompt variable for stability.

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I3.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I3.i4.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I3.i4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Improve
  ($`\iota_{\mathrm{tg}}`$).</span>
  $`\iota_{\mathrm{tg}}:\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\text{evo}}`$
  rewrites prompt variables via a textual-gradient-descent step: it
  constructs an update instruction from each variable’s role
  description, current value, and accumulated textual gradients, then
  queries an optimizer LLM and extracts the improved variable text from
  a constrained output format.

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I3.i5"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I3.i5.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I3.i5.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Commit
  ($`\kappa_{\mathrm{tg}}`$).</span>
  $`\kappa_{\mathrm{tg}}:\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\text{evo}}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}`$
  synchronizes the updated prompt variables back into the running agent
  and clears caches, completing the state transition.

  </div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS5.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A5.SS3.SSS5.p3.7.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">The
Evolutionary Loop.</span> Algorithm <a href="#alg2" class="ltx_ref"
title="Algorithm 2 ‣ E.3.5 TextGrad Optimizer ‣ E.3 Layer 2: Self-Evolution Protocol Layer ‣ Appendix E Details of Self-Evolution Protocol ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">2</span></a> presents the full TextGrad
optimization cycle in operator form. At each iteration, the agent is
executed under the current prompt variables to obtain a trace
$`\mathcal{Z}`$ via $`\chi_{\mathrm{tg}}`$, an LLM-based evaluator
produces a natural-language critique $`g\in\mathcal{G}_{\mathrm{tg}}`$
via $`\lambda_{\mathrm{tg}}`$, the critique is assigned as a *textual
gradient* to the optimizable prompt variables via
$`\beta_{\mathrm{tg}}`$, the prompt variables are improved via
$`\iota_{\mathrm{tg}}`$ using textual-gradient-descent, and the
candidate state is committed via $`\kappa_{\mathrm{tg}}`$ to synchronize
the updated prompts back into the running agent (and clear caches)
before the next iteration.

</div>

class="ltx_float ltx_float_algorithm ltx_framed ltx_framed_top">

0: Setup</span>

<div id="alg2.l2" class="ltx_listingline">
<span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_listingline"><span id="alg2.l2.2.1.1"
class="ltx_text" style="font-size:80%;">2:</span></span> Set backward
engine to <span class="math inline"><em>M</em><sub>eval</sub></span>
<span id="alg2.l2.1" class="ltx_text" style="float:right;"> <span
id="alg2.l2.1.1" class="ltx_inline-block ltx_parbox ltx_align_top"

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS6" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">E.3.6 </span>Reinforce++ Optimizer

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS6.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A5.SS3.SSS6.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Evolvable
Variables.</span> Reinforce++ optimizes a trainable subset of RSPL
resources, focusing on prompt variables and tool implementations (native
scripts, MCP tools \[<a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Equipping agents for the real world with agent skills">1</a>\],
and agent skills \[<a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Introduction to agent skills">2</a>\]), and optionally refining
the produced solution text. Our implementation follows a two stage
structure: (i) update trainable variables that govern behavior (e.g.,
prompts and tools), and (ii) update the solution itself when enabled.

</div>

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS6.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A5.SS3.SSS6.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Operator
Algebra.</span> Reinforce++ is characterized by a clipped objective with
an explicit penalty to a reference solution, while using reflection to
translate RL signals into concrete edits. We group the method into a
small set of core operators:

- <span id="A5.I4.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I4.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I4.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Sample
  ($`\chi_{\mathrm{rpp}}`$).</span>
  $`\chi_{\mathrm{rpp}}:(A,\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}},x,f)\rightarrow\mathcal{Z}`$
  samples a rollout under the current resources and yields an execution
  trace containing the produced answer.

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I4.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I4.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I4.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Reward
  ($`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rpp}}`$).</span>
  $`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{rpp}}:(y^{(t)},y^{(t-1)},y^{*},y_{\mathrm{sft}})\rightarrow(r^{(t)},A^{(t)},J^{(t)},\pi^{(t)})`$
  computes the RL signal tuple from the current solution $`y^{(t)}`$.
  Here $`r^{(t)}`$ is a task reward comparing $`y^{(t)}`$ with
  $`y^{*}`$, and $`\pi^{(t)}`$ is a policy ratio surrogate approximated
  via text similarity $`\eta(\cdot,\cdot)`$ as
  $`\pi^{(t)}\triangleq\eta(y^{(t-1)},y^{(t)})`$ (since token-level
  probability ratios are unavailable in inference-only LLM settings). We
  define a penalty to a reference solution $`y_{\mathrm{sft}}`$ as
  $`\mathrm{pen}^{(t)}\triangleq\beta\,\bigl|\log\max(\eta(y_{\mathrm{sft}},y^{(t)}),\epsilon_{0})\bigr|`$
  and set $`A^{(t)}\triangleq r^{(t)}-\mathrm{pen}^{(t)}`$. The clipped
  Reinforce++ objective is

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  | 
  ``` math
  J^{(t)}\triangleq\min\bigl(\pi^{(t)}A^{(t)},\;\bar{\pi}^{(t)}A^{(t)}\bigr),\quad\bar{\pi}^{(t)}\triangleq\mathrm{clip}(\pi^{(t)},1-\epsilon,1+\epsilon).
  ``` |  |

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I4.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I4.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I4.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Diagnose
  ($`\delta_{\mathrm{rpp}}`$).</span>
  $`\delta_{\mathrm{rpp}}:(\mathcal{Z},\mathcal{V}_{\text{train}},r^{(t)},A^{(t)},J^{(t)},\pi^{(t)})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}`$
  produces an edit oriented diagnosis that is explicitly conditioned on
  the RL metrics and the execution trace.

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I4.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I4.i4.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I4.i4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Improve
  ($`\iota_{\mathrm{rpp}}`$).</span>
  $`\iota_{\mathrm{rpp}}:(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\text{evo}}`$
  applies RL informed edits to either (i) the trainable resources
  $`\mathcal{V}_{\text{train}}`$ such as prompts and tools, or (ii) the
  solution variable itself when solution refinement is enabled, yielding
  a candidate state.

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I4.i5"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I4.i5.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I4.i5.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Commit
  ($`\kappa_{\mathrm{rpp}}`$).</span>
  $`\kappa_{\mathrm{rpp}}:\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\text{evo}}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}`$
  applies accepted updates back to RSPL resources, completing the state
  transition.

  </div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS6.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A5.SS3.SSS6.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">The
Evolutionary Loop.</span> Algorithm <a href="#alg3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Algorithm 3 ‣ E.3.6 Reinforce++ Optimizer ‣ E.3 Layer 2: Self-Evolution Protocol Layer ‣ Appendix E Details of Self-Evolution Protocol ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">3</span></a> summarizes the Reinforce++
loop in a phased form. Each iteration (i) computes Reinforce++ signals
via the clipped objective and the penalty to the reference solution,
(ii) improves trainable resources through RL conditioned reflection and
edits, (iii) optionally improves the solution text, and (iv) applies an
early stopping evaluation.

</div>

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS7" class="section ltx_subsubsection">

#### <span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_subsubsection">E.3.7 </span>GRPO Optimizer

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS7.p1" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A5.SS3.SSS7.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Evolvable
Variables.</span> GRPO optimizes a trainable subset of RSPL resources,
focusing on prompt variables and tool implementations (native scripts,
MCP tools \[<a href="#bib.bib4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Equipping agents for the real world with agent skills">1</a>\],
and agent skills \[<a href="#bib.bib3" class="ltx_ref"
title="Introduction to agent skills">2</a>\]), and optionally refining
the produced solution text. Similar to Reinforce++, our implementation
follows a two stage structure: (i) update trainable variables that
govern behavior (e.g., prompts and tools), and (ii) update the solution
itself when enabled.

</div>

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS7.p2" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A5.SS3.SSS7.p2.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Operator
Algebra.</span> GRPO is characterized by sampling multiple candidate
solutions per step and using group normalized advantages with a clipped
objective. We formalize the method with the following core operators:

- <span id="A5.I5.i1"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I5.i1.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I5.i1.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Sample
  ($`\chi_{\mathrm{grpo}}`$).</span>
  $`\chi_{\mathrm{grpo}}:(A,\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}},x,f,K)\rightarrow\{\mathcal{Z}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{K}`$
  samples $`K`$ independent rollouts under the current resources,
  yielding $`K`$ execution traces each containing a candidate solution
  $`y_{i}`$.

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I5.i2"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I5.i2.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I5.i2.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Reward
  ($`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{grpo}}`$).</span>
  $`\varepsilon_{\mathrm{grpo}}:(\{y_{i}\}_{i=1}^{K},y^{*},y^{(t-1)})\rightarrow(\{r_{i}\}_{i=1}^{K},\{A_{i}\}_{i=1}^{K},\{J_{i}\}_{i=1}^{K},\{\pi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{K})`$
  computes RL signals for all $`K`$ candidates. For each candidate
  $`y_{i}`$, we compute a task reward $`r_{i}`$ comparing $`y_{i}`$ with
  $`y^{*}`$, a policy ratio surrogate
  $`\pi_{i}\triangleq\eta(y^{(t-1)},y_{i})`$ approximated via text
  similarity $`\eta(\cdot,\cdot)`$ (since token-level probability ratios
  are unavailable in inference-only LLM settings), and a group
  normalized advantage $`A_{i}`$ by normalizing rewards across the
  candidate set: $`A_{i}=(r_{i}-\bar{r})/\sigma_{r}`$ where $`\bar{r}`$
  and $`\sigma_{r}`$ are the mean and standard deviation of
  $`\{r_{i}\}_{i=1}^{K}`$. The GRPO clipped objective for each candidate
  is

  |  |  |  |
  |----|----|----|
  |  | 
  ``` math
  J_{i}\triangleq\min\bigl(\pi_{i}A_{i},\;\bar{\pi}_{i}A_{i}\bigr),\quad\bar{\pi}_{i}\triangleq\begin{cases}\min(\pi_{i},1+\epsilon)&\text{if }A_{i}\geq 0\\
  \max(\pi_{i},1-\epsilon)&\text{if }A_{i}<0\end{cases}.
  ``` |  |

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I5.i3"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I5.i3.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I5.i3.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Diagnose
  ($`\delta_{\mathrm{grpo}}`$).</span>
  $`\delta_{\mathrm{grpo}}:(\{\mathcal{Z}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{K},\mathcal{V}_{\text{train}},\{r_{i},A_{i},J_{i},\pi_{i}\}_{i=1}^{K})\rightarrow\mathcal{H}`$
  produces an edit oriented diagnosis that is explicitly conditioned on
  the multiple candidate solutions and their RL metrics, enabling the
  optimizer to identify patterns across candidates.

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I5.i4"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I5.i4.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I5.i4.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Improve
  ($`\iota_{\mathrm{grpo}}`$).</span>
  $`\iota_{\mathrm{grpo}}:(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{H})\rightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\text{evo}}`$
  applies RL informed edits to either (i) the trainable resources
  $`\mathcal{V}_{\text{train}}`$ such as prompts and tools, or (ii) the
  solution variable itself when solution refinement is enabled, yielding
  a candidate state.

  </div>
- <span id="A5.I5.i5"><span class="ltx_tag ltx_tag_item">•</span></span>
  <div id="A5.I5.i5.p1" class="ltx_para">

  <span id="A5.I5.i5.p1.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">Commit
  ($`\kappa_{\mathrm{grpo}}`$).</span>
  $`\kappa_{\mathrm{grpo}}:\mathcal{V}^{\prime}_{\text{evo}}\rightarrow\mathcal{V}_{\text{evo}}`$
  applies accepted updates back to RSPL resources, completing the state
  transition.

  </div>

</div>

<div id="A5.SS3.SSS7.p3" class="ltx_para">

<span id="A5.SS3.SSS7.p3.1.1" class="ltx_text ltx_font_bold">The
Evolutionary Loop.</span> Algorithm <a href="#alg4" class="ltx_ref"
title="Algorithm 4 ‣ E.3.7 GRPO Optimizer ‣ E.3 Layer 2: Self-Evolution Protocol Layer ‣ Appendix E Details of Self-Evolution Protocol ‣ Autogenesis: A Self-Evolving Agent Protocol"><span
class="ltx_text ltx_ref_tag">4</span></a> summarizes the GRPO loop in a
phased form. Each iteration (i) samples $`K`$ candidate solutions, (ii)
computes GRPO signals via group normalized advantages and clipped
objectives, (iii) improves trainable resources through multi candidate
conditioned reflection and edits, (iv) optionally improves the solution
text, and (v) applies an early stopping evaluation.

</div>

<figure id="alg4"
class="ltx_float ltx_float_algorithm ltx_framed ltx_framed_top">
<div id="alg4.3" class="ltx_listing">
